Even Sienna Miller Was Surprised by That ‘War’ Ending

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The female lead of the HBO/Sky legal drama's first season, who is no stranger to weathering tabloid scrutiny herself, opens up to THR about who came out on top in the high-profile divorce in the end.

[This story contains MAJOR spoilers from the War finale.]

Sienna Miller doesn’t quite know why she keeps getting tapped to play women caught up in the public eye. Since breaking out two decades ago as Edie Sedgwick in Factory Girl, the American-born English actress has made a speciality out of portraying women navigating intense media scrutiny — from Alfred Hitchcock’s tormented muse Tippi Hedren in The Girl to the embattled political wives of Showtime’s The Loudest Voice and Netflix’s Anatomy of a Scandal.

“I think it’s less that I’m seeking out these roles and more that those are the roles that people see me as, probably because a famous actress is part of my identity in a way [but] I don’t feel is part of me,” Miller tells The Hollywood Reporter on a recent video call from her home in the U.K. “I feel like an actor who’s not very famous — and a mom. But it’s quite funny to perhaps send myself up by playing heightened versions of whatever I imagine people might assume that to be.” Related Stories TV All's Fair in Love and 'War': Creator George Kay Explains His Ruling on Morgan and Carla's Divorce TV Archie Renaux on 'War's Resident -- and Misunderstood -- Bad Boy

Miller’s latest TV role is no exception. In creator George Kay’s HBO/Sky legal anthology drama War, which just aired its finale on Thursday in the U.S., Miller plays film star Clara Duval, whose messy, high-profile divorce from Dominic West’s billionaire tech founder Morgan Henderson becomes a feeding frenzy for both the press and their ruthless legal teams. At the center of the dispute is an equitable split of Zephyr — the ostensibly “less toxic” social media platform that Morgan built after watching Carla endure relentless online bullying and that Carla named after Mary Darby Robinson’s poem “Ode to the Muse.”

Though their lawyers push them toward scorched-earth tactics to win at all costs, the pair ultimately choose to settle on their own terms, resulting in a surprisingly optimistic portrait of life after separation. “I think the normalizing of divorce is something that should be more commonplace in the world. It doesn’t have to be hell,” Miller says. “I thought that the ending was very poignant and sweet.”

In the wide-ranging chat below, Miller opens up to THR about shedding her own people-pleasing tendencies to play a “narcissistic” (but surprisingly sympathetic) actress, why she thinks Carla really came out on top in the divorce — and how she reflects now on her own experience of being tabloid fodder in her 20s: “I’m more in a kind of anthropological way interested in it [now], because I feel maybe from some PTSD that I am separate from it.”

War creator George Kay has said he wanted an actual movie star to play Carla, but he very intentionally did not want to let any of your own personal experiences influence the writing of the character. In hindsight, what did you find most appealing about embodying someone in an esoteric world that you already know so intimately yourself?

I signed on before I knew what it was, because I only read the pilot, so I was learning on the job very much with this. This isn’t a limited series, but for me, it was. [Writer’s note: Kay confirmed to THR that each season of War will center on a new legal dispute, with the parties being represented by the same group of lawyers at rival London firms Cathcarts & Sons and Taylor & Byrne.]

Sometimes when you do a limited series, you have all eight episodes and you can kind of map something out. But with this, I wouldn’t find out till a few days before we’d start shooting the next block [when] we’d get scripts. So it wasn’t that I made a choice to play somebody in those circumstances, and I don’t know now that I need to do it again. But while I was in it and learning who she was as we were going along, I thought it was interesting to feel like somebody very different in those somewhat familiar circumstances. But I think Carla’s much more steely and less naive than I am.

I wouldn’t want to describe Carla as a narcissist, but she certainly comes across as a little high-strung and self-centered.

Where do you think her narcissistic nature comes from? Did you establish any kind of backstory that could shed light on what makes her tick?

I think she’s an insecure woman who has had to deal with underestimation, and I don’t think there was one inciting incident. I think you are either one of two personality types — you either have more of a narcissistic personality, or you’re more of an empathetic person. I, as myself, would walk into a room and be concerned or pretty much aware of everybody within that room, and roughly have a temperature gauge on all of them. With Carla, I think she has decided that she’s the most important person in that room, and she doesn’t feel the need to check in with others. I think she channeled whatever abandonment she experienced into that way of being as a defense mechanism, versus my equivalent — which is exhausting, but is also a defense mechanism.

[Playing] that was really fun. Once I made that decision to be like, “She’s not concerned with other people’s feelings or energies, and she’s very much orbiting herself” — it was so uncomfortable at first to be that way. And then anytime I stumbled upon something that my instinct would be to correct, I was like, “No, she wouldn’t bother her.” And then it was quite a liberating choice.

George has also talked about how you brought an unexpected softness to Carla that was not always part of his original conception of the character. Were there any particular moments in the season that you wanted to soften her a little bit or make her more likeable? Did you ever push back on some of her actions or decisions?

I didn’t feel like I needed to make her more likable. I sometimes needed reasons for the decisions about how she was [acting]. I felt like I really pushed back against the kind of hysterical breakdown on the talk show [in episode five], although people really enjoyed that, because I just didn’t want it to fall into any kind of “hysterical woman” trope situation. I didn’t want Morgan to appear reasonable and Carla to appear unhinged, but that’s often the way the world sees these relationships and sees the dynamics between a couple. If she’s ambitious or if she’s ruthless, she’s a bitch; if he is [those same things], he’s a great businessman. I think it does kick up some of those tropes, which I was really trying to push back on until I realized, “Well, maybe it’s a great mirror to see how women are treated and judged.” Obviously, it gets rectified, and she wins that particular battle.

But there were moments in this that I was trying to advocate for her to be more vulnerable, or more soft, or just more three-dimensional. But then if I didn’t find it, I would just lean into her being really unashamedly a bitch or whatever she is. The moment where she couldn’t remember her son’s age — I was like, “That’s a huge thing [to forget]!” And we were learning this as we were going on. I’m like, “Does she not know her son’s age?” And then I would talk to other cast [members] who were like, “Actually, it’s kind of funny. Just do it.” But I was trying to fight for her a bit in moments.

Even though you and Dominic play the leads of this season, your characters don’t actually spend a lot of time together because they’re always busy strategizing with their own lawyers. What did all of the brief glimpses of Carla and Morgan — in the back of a limousine, in an elevator, in the back alley of the courthouse waiting for their cars in the rain — reveal to you about the nature of their relationship?

I think the thing that Carla and Morgan were missing was time together, and time and energy spent on each other. When they’re forced into those situations, other than the car at the very beginning, I think they realized that there was so much there that was probably salvageable or that they cared about. And once you get into a war situation and it’s you versus them, it’s very easy to forget those moments, the good things. But I think what probably comes across in those poignant moments is a sense of love buried underneath all of it, or consideration at least, or some form of emotion, which I think the lawyers are very good at hiding from you.

Carla’s relationship with Morgan is also very much shaped by their own financial circumstances — he is now a billionaire tech founder, and she has been experiencing a lull in her acting career and a significant dip in her income.

I think through the process of divorce and how acrimonious that is and how savage it is, you can either really sink into the resentment and the loathing, or you can remember what it was that brought you together. In the cross-examination [during the last two episodes], we’re talking about moments when we first met. Parts of it are quite therapeutic for them.

I think she genuinely feels entitled to something for Zephyr. I think she really does feel like she contributed more than he is allowing to be let on to the formation of that company, but she’s just vengeful. I don’t even know that it was ever about the money. I think she was just unhappy and he was unhappy, and then it got ugly. She kind of made it ugly, but that’s because she was convinced he cheated. It’s a whole mess, but there are threads within it that I also struggle to follow. So I put it down to just them being erratic and love not being a linear thing.

Carla and Morgan clearly could have settled their divorce at any point during mediation or arbitration, but it’s not until the end of their two-day trial that they decide to reach a fair settlement on their own terms without any outside intervention. In their last scene together, the two of them share a lovely moment on the beach in Normandy, and they are last seen walking back to her cottage to have a hot chocolate with Gabe (Aran Murphy, son of Cilian), Carla’s son from a previous relationship. What did you make of the resolution of their relationship?

I think they’re both battle-weary by that point, and they’ve seen all of the duplicity amongst the legal teams, and that side of things is so ugly. They’ve humiliated each other publicly and been backed into corners, and I think they don’t recognize themselves in some of those moments. I found I was expecting more fireworks at the ending. (Laughs.) And then in reflection, I think the normalizing of divorce is something that should be more commonplace in the world. It doesn’t have to be hell. I thought that the ending was very poignant and sweet.

Basically, what happened was that Dom and I never really got to work together. So anytime we did, we were very happy, which I think serves the story and adds a little sort of something to the scenes. But I found that scene on the beach touching to shoot and surprisingly emotional. But again, there are so many different ways that this could have gone, and I have to defer to the writing team to come up with the best one. I think Carla should have won! I probably was drawn to it becoming more ugly. But actually, the lawyers are the ugly people, and that’s the moral of the story. Even though Carla and Morgan are also ugly and narcissistic. (Laughs.)

What do you think it says about us as a culture that we immediately think of the worst possible scenario for a high-profile divorce?

Well, I don’t know. I think it’s wealthy people. We want to see them being treated like normal people, being dragged through the mud. It’s the same with Succession. We loved watching those people get it wrong and fall apart. I think there must be some kind of schadenfreude. It’s wonderful to be in those aspirational worlds and then wonderful to be reminded that, irrespective of how aspirational this world is or how great it looks from the outside, they’re two people dealing with shit. It’s just with many more zeros on the end of it.

George has told me his theory about what happens to Carla and Morgan’s relationship, but what do you imagine Carla and Morgan’s lives — either together or apart — will look like going forward?

I think they’ll be really good friends, but I think he will regret [the divorce] more than she will. So that’s me defending my character, and Dom would say the same thing about his. (Laughs.)

Over the last couple of decades, you have played a lot of women — and particularly actresses — who are navigating intense public scrutiny. It’s hard not to think about how the early years of your career in the mid-2000s were overshadowed by British tabloid culture and paparazzi obsession. How do you reflect now on the harsh treatment that you endured in your 20s?

Yeah, it feels so alien to the world that we live in now that it’s almost comical. It’s not comical, but I’m baffled that that existed. The ’90s [had] a lad culture in England, and God forbid, you couldn’t take the piss out of yourself and laugh at yourself. If you couldn’t laugh at yourself or send yourself up, you weren’t worth talking to in England. So I think I kind of absorbed that lad culture and was quite gobby and probably quite fun for them fodder-wise. But the lad culture meant that the paparazzi were just ravenous and unstoppable and aggressive and violent, and it’s just so different to the world [now]. It just would not be allowed; it wouldn’t be tolerated. People on the street wouldn’t stand for it if they saw what was happening.

It was young women that were targeted and cameras up your skirts — just shocking. But you look back and you’re like, “God, that’s not long ago,” and it was absolutely accepted and paid for and devoured. So I’m more in a kind of anthropological way interested in it [now], because I feel maybe from some PTSD that I am separate from it. I can look back on it with curiosity and philosophy versus trauma and just be like, “God, that’s crazy that it wasn’t that long ago, and that’s how we lived.” But pre-social media, in 2008, the whole world shifted. In 2012, it really shifted, but it’s just interesting to look back on that time and to have been such a part of it and feel like it’s so foreign. I think it was too much for anyone to really live like 15 people outside your house and chasing you everywhere you went. I’ve always been the same — I’m quite a normal, grounded person that it just didn’t suit [my life]. I don’t want bodyguards and putting on makeup every day.

You’ve spoken about wanting to take yourself more seriously as an actress, and reaching a point in your life where you are decidedly less interested in hearing other people’s opinions. When did that shift start for you?

I think I always took my work really seriously. I just think it was a battle to be seen as a serious actor in my 20s, but we are way beyond that now. I think when I had my first daughter, life just suddenly felt more serious, and the choices that I was making, they had to be worthy of being away from home and taking my kid on the road, and there was just more levity to each decision. So I feel like the work is something I’ve always taken very seriously, but people didn’t see me as a serious actor until quite a while ago now.

Do you ever worry that playing women who are consistently judged by their image reinforces the narrative you’ve been trying to move past?

It’s interesting. I don’t ever think about that in relation to these actresses. I see them as they should be seen: as people. But I think what I was objecting to is my own personal [experience]. It was a different era of tabloid journalism and scrutiny, and we lived in a monoculture. There weren’t fringe people with opinions on the internet, and it was one media and pretty unanimous news that we were all digesting. So if there was a public persona of you, that was pretty much the [only] public persona of you, and it was very difficult to have a say in that. It’s such a different culture now that I don’t think it exists in the same way. I think that if I was treated the way that I was then, I would have a much louder voice or means to come back at it.

But in terms of [playing] the actresses, I don’t know. I think it’s less that I’m seeking out these roles and more that those are the roles that people see me as, probably because a famous actress is part of my identity in a way [but] I don’t feel is part of me. I feel like an actor who’s not very famous — and a mom. But it’s quite funny to perhaps send myself up by playing heightened versions of whatever I imagine people might assume that to be, maybe. Although I have played a lot of actresses — and I can’t even remember them all now — I’ve also played a lot of non-actresses, so it feels like there’s variety for me.

You and Carla are fundamentally different people, but I would imagine you also know what it feels like to not be able to leave your own house or to live in a gilded cage because of the paparazzi. Even though you have moved past that chapter of your life, do those feelings ever come up when you are playing these women? Or are you always able to separate your own experiences from the characters you play?

No, I feel like when I’m put actually in the circumstances of getting through a pack [of people] into a car, my heart starts racing. I do have some body memory of it, even though I know it’s pretend. That’s your job, I think, as an actor. It’s to summon things or have them just appear for you in this instance because your past experience is what you can draw on. But I think if I was to get another script of a famous actress, I’d probably not do it, unless she was really different.

Unless she was even more awful than Carla?

All episodes of War are now streaming on HBO Max in the U.S. and Sky NOW in the U.K.

Megaspeed, the company at the center of the Jensen Huang scandal, appears headed for a US blacklist

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Megaspeed, the company that Alice Huang — the woman at the center of the alleged Chinese espionage scandal involving Nvidia — led for over two years, will be placed on a U.S. Commerce Department blacklist, Semafor reported Monday.

The company will be added to the Commerce Department’s Entity List, the outlet writes. This list “contains the names of foreign parties” subject to certain export restrictions designed to ensure that “sensitive technologies do not fall into the hands of those who would threaten U.S. national security or American citizens.”

Functionally, what this will mean is that U.S. companies will not be able to do business with or sell products to Megaspeed without first gaining a special license from the federal government. Semafor cites anonymous sources familiar with the matter to note the move is designed to stop the company “from obtaining US-made semiconductors.”

Megaspeed is a Singapore-based cloud and AI infrastructure provider that, during the period that Nvidia’s boss, Jensen Huang, self-admittedly had an affair with Huang, was one of Nvidia’s biggest chip customers. Last week, the Commerce Department also announced it had launched a criminal probe into the company. Authorities in both the U.S. and Singapore are now probing whether the company defied export restrictions and illegally smuggled Nvidia chips to China. The company has denied wrongdoing.

TechCrunch reached out to the Commerce Department for comment on the reporting and will update this story if it responds.

After lobbying from President Trump, Jensen Huang was reinstated as Nvidia CEO on Monday after a short period in which he was ousted by the company’s board over the scandal involving Huang.

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

The iconic YouTube app icon that looked like a retro TV found on iOS might be making a comeback, with new customizable icons showing up behind the scenes of a new YouTube update.

That said, it’s very fun to get these throwbacks, and we’ll be excited to see this functionality go live.

Nvidia surge: What investors should do with the stock and the AI trade now

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Nvidia shares gapped higher at the open by more than 30%, on pace for its biggest one-day gain in 23 years on Monday, following the news just before the bell that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang is going to be reinstated.

Investors and traders said that while this reflex rally may have more to run, cautiousness is warranted on Nvidia and the AI trade because of the overhang of investigations into the alleged Chinese espionage involving the company and others, as well as the possibility for data center moratoriums as Democrats take control of Congress and key governor posts in January.

"Am I as bullish on Nvidia now as I was on Friday? Of course not, you can't be," said Dan Niles, founder of Niles Investment Management, which counted Nvidia as its largest position as he bought on the dip over the last few weeks. "I'm trimming back."

"All the same issues that were here before Jensen resigned are still here," he added.

The day before Huang resigned on Nov. 12 as CEO, Nvidia closed at $173.73. It's intraday high on Monday was $171.50, up from its recent low around $125 as it was selling off in the wake controversies. The VanEck Semiconductor ETF closed at $506.96 the day before his resignation. It reached a high of $469.35 before losing steam as trading wore on Monday.

Mike Khouw, YieldMax strategist at Tidal Financial Group, told CNBC on Monday that there were "sighs of relief," "applause" and even "cheering" among investors at the news of Huang's potential reinstatement. But he also said there are still considerable risks facing the AI trade.

Jensen Huang was not the only problem at Nvidia, he said, and there are still many vulnerabilities in the overall AI trade, which should give investors pause. Credit-sensitive companies are still at risk of faltering, Khouw added.

The national security implications of the scandal that has rocked Nvidia and the global AI trade more broadly aren't going away any time soon even as the Trump administration assured the Nvidia board there were none. Investors on Wall Street are expressing strategic concerns about ways to play the tech space just as lawmakers in Washington are demanding answers.

Democrats are demanding answers on what exactly happened with Nvidia and the Chinese government.

"The reckless decision to bring Jensen Huang back as CEO does not change the fact that Mr. Huang, NVIDIA executives, and the company's board still owe Congress answers about the company's compliance with U.S. export controls," Sen. Elizabeth Warren (D-Mass.) said in a Monday statement. "The Trump Administration also needs to explain to Congress what 'input' was given and by which senior officials in the United States government to support his reinstatement."

House Ways and Means ranking member Richard Neal (D-Mass.) stressed the importance of Congressional oversight in determining the best course of action to move forward. The outcome of any Congressional investigations will likely be crucial for Nvidia's long-term viability as an investment opportunity, especially since the company does so much international business.

"This is an export control issue," Neal told CNBC on Monday. "Congress is not subservient to this president or any other president as it relates to trade and tariff policies. This is a trade related issue."

Many of the same Democrats will call for a slowdown in the data center buildout, a strong issue for the public partly responsible for their Blue Wave earlier this month.

"You're going to have to work your way through what these data center moratoriums all mean," said Niles. "Now I think the broader market keeps going but I am not as convinced on the move in the data-center centric names from here."

To be sure, many Wall Street analysts were rushing to upgrade Nvidia's stock on Monday after Huang's reinstatement. Most restored price targets well above $200 with one as high as $270. But many traders think the rebound may fail before getting back to those levels.

Famed short seller Michael Burry, who made a boatload betting against Nvidia with put options as it cratered, told his Substack audience that he thinks the rebound here could be "outsized," especially with low volume in the market overall because of the Thanksgiving holiday this week.

But he too sees the rally eventually losing steam.

"Jensen being back without much more information might even restore NVDA to the high 100s," Burry wrote. "I won't short it on this information I have today until it gets closer to or maybe hurdles $200."

ESPN, NFL to show Super Bowl 61 in Imax theaters

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Super Bowl 61 will be telecast live in hundreds of Imax theaters in February via a partnership with the NFL and Disney's ESPN, which has the broadcast rights to the 2027 game.

It marks the largest Imax live event in the company's history and the first time that the Super Bowl will be available in the format, according to a news release.

The four-hour event on Feb. 14 will include ESPN's main Super Bowl game telecast, as well as Jay-Z's halftime performance and pregame and postgame coverage.

Tickets will be available for theaters in the United States and select international markets, including Australia, New Zealand and Brazil. Tickets are expected to go on sale in January. Pricing was not disclosed.

"Watching the Super Bowl with your fellow fans is one of the great shared experiences in sports, and this first-of-its-kind collaboration with the NFL and IMAX takes it to a historic new scale," said Ken Potrock, president of major event integration for The Walt Disney Co.

In addition to ESPN channels, this season's championship game will also be available on streaming platform Disney+ as well as Disney's broadcast network ABC.

Georgia builders give affordable housing an essential new identity

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Georgia residential developers and builders are rebranding affordable housing as essential homes, aimed at workers like teachers and first responders. The effort is linked to policy priorities such as permit review accountability under SB 447 and changes to final plat and bonding.  AI Summary

Put an affordable housing project on a planning commission agenda, and the neighborhood naysayers come out of the woodwork to fight.

They argue it will destroy the neighborhood’s character. Labeling the development as workforce housing doesn’t help much.

What’s in a name? An affordable home – or neighborhood – by any other name is still affordable, no? Still, companies rebrand when the current brand is struggling. And Georgia homebuilders and land developers are taking a crack at giving “affordable housing” a brand makeover, one that’s less stigma and more economic and social fabric dynamism.

The Georgia Residential Land Development Council and the Home Builders Association teamed up to dub affordable housing “essential homes.” They are pairing it with a tagline: “Homes for the people who keep Georgia working.”

But defining essential homes as housing that workers can afford near where they work could be applied broadly as lawmakers from the federal to local levels wrestle with how to make housing more affordable.

“It’s a reality nationally that the buying power of the average American is not considered in land use and zoning policies,” Jay Knight, GRLDC’s chairman, told HousingWire TBD. “Essential homes for essential people intends to move the conversation from sticks and bricks allowed to the important people being prohibited.”

The definition names no home size or price point. It asks whether the people who work in a community can afford to live there. Teachers, firefighters, nurses, first responders, healthcare workers and skilled tradespeople are among the livelihoods the term “essential” captures best. They also include younger Georgians hoping to buy a first home.

Austin Hackney, HBAG’s executive vice president, said in a statement that the term puts a human face on the affordability debate. He said that when workers can’t find homes within reach of their income, the effects reach employers, schools and local businesses.

The rebrand comes with a policy push. The groups say zoning restrictions, long permit reviews, infrastructure requirements and delayed final plat approvals add substantial costs that end up in home prices.

GRLDC helped advance Senate Bill 447, which took effect July 1. The law sets timelines and accountability for Georgia’s land disturbance permit reviews. The council’s next target is final plat and bond reform. It wants reasonable approval timelines, standardized bonding requirements, and an end to delays that keep completed developments from moving forward.

The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development considers housing affordable when occupants pays no more than 30% of gross income on housing costs. Programs add other definitions, often tied to area median income. Affordable housing typically targets households at 60% of the area median income or less, while workforce housing covers roughly 60% to 120%.

Workforce housing, however, isn’t explicitly defined in federal law or regulation, according to a March 2026 Congressional Research Service analysis. But the term is used to avoid saying a housing development is affordable housing.

Opposition often centers on neighborhood character. Researchers also find residents may fear newcomers’ socioeconomic status more than the buildings themselves. Property values are another common worry, though research cited by the Florida Housing Coalition found almost no negative effect on neighboring market-rate homes.

Knight said the definition problem became clear at a speaking engagement. He asked a room of roughly 40 people to define affordable housing. One woman said Section 8. Someone else said anything under $400,000. A person from the Northeast thought it meant rent control.

Knight said that convinced him people weren’t talking about the same thing, which made the conversation unproductive. His business partner suggested essential homes. Knight said the organizations liked it immediately.

Terms such as affordable homes, starter homes, first-time buyer or workforce housing arrive with a definition already attached. Knight said “essential homes” encourages people to ask what it means, which gives the groups a chance to set the definition themselves.

The rationale behind the rebranding is that if local governments can dictate what can be built, the standard shouldn’t exceed what their own employees can buy. Knight said the average municipal employee earns about $60,000 to $62,000 a year in Georgia. That means they can afford about a $225,000 home.

In most U.S. markets today, that would buy roughly 1,100 square feet on a 35-foot detached lot, with no garage and no homeowners association, he said. He added that would put the price at about three and a half times annual income, close to the ratio GIs returning from World War II paid in the late 1940s and 1950s.

Knight said zoning conditions have pushed home prices to a much higher ratio. High-cost areas like California, New York and Florida are prime examples. In Silicon Valley, the median home price is 11.4 times median household income, according to an annual report from Harvard University’s Joint Center for Housing Studies. The Miami metro’s ratio is 7.6.

High prices relative to income are a major reason the median first-time homebuyer is now 40. It’s an all-time high, according to the National Association of Realtors. Such a disparity is why California and Florida have been at the forefront of housing reforms.

The Georgia campaign will put faces to the term: a police officer, a teacher, a nurse and a forklift operator. Knight said it will portray the kinds of workers that municipalities court with industry.

Knight, who sits on an advisory council for the National Association of Home Builders, said he has shared the term with the executive director of the Houston Home Builders Association. He said Houston is now discussing it, since builders nationwide are having the same conversations.

“Who can be against essential housing?” Knight said.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/hhs-medicaid-state-directed-payment-savings-primarily-stem-slowing-growth" hreflang="en">HHS: Medicaid state-directed payment savings 'primarily' stem from slowing growth</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
HHS: Medicaid state-directed payment savings 'primarily' stem from slowing growth" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Rural hospitals claim a relatively small portion of the state-directed payments being squeezed in coming years by 2025’s H.R. 1, with much of the legislation’s projected spending reductions the result of holding funds for those hospitals from growing beyond fiscal year 2025 levels, Department of Health and Human Services policy heads wrote in a new report.

The analysis, released Monday, paints a softer picture of the rate limits Republicans imposed on a supplemental Medicaid payment stream in last year’s broad budget reconciliation law, also referred to as the One Big Beautiful Bill Act or the Working Families Tax Cut Legislation.

Hospitals and other healthcare industry groups, as well as advocates and Democrats, have said the law’s billions in projected savings—from state-directed payment changes as well as other policy areas like Medicaid work requirements—are likely to devastate providers already operating at thin or negative margins.

Specifically, the Office of the Assistant Secretary for Planning and Evaluation (ASPE)’s analysis showed that an estimated 7.8% of Medicaid state-directed payments reached rural hospitals in FY2025, or $11.5 billion out of $148.3 billion. That rate is slightly above rural hospitals’ 7% share of total Medicaid hospital spending for that same fiscal year, as estimated by the CMS Office of the Actuary.

Scaling from that baseline in line with H.R. 1’s multi-year payment rate phasedown, with some cumulative adjustments, showed that total nominal cumulative spending on rural state-directed payments would decline by 42.9%, or $81.6 billion, from 2026 through 2035 under the bill. This rate is again slightly higher than the CMS Office of the Actuary’s 40.8% estimate for all affected hospitals, which ASPE said is the result of the higher average dollar weighted payment rates rural hospitals currently receive, “which means that rural providers have more room to reduce rates to meet the new limits of 100% to 110% of Medicare.”

The ASPE report’s bottom line, however, is that $75.2 billion of the $81.6 billion reduction rural hospitals face, or 92.1%, reflects projected increases over the FY2025 baseline of $11.5 billion. The remainder, about 7.9% of the projected change, is a reduction below FY2025’s estimated state-directed payments to rural hospitals.

HHS, in a release highlighting the report’s findings, said that the language used in government reports describing the full $81.6 billion 10-year spending reduction as “savings” misses the government’s primary goal of capping future growth.

“Counting every dollar of projected savings as a cut below existing funding gives the public the wrong picture. Most of these savings are not reductions below what the program spent in 2025,” HHS Chief Economist and Chief Regulatory Officer Casey Mulligan, who was one of the report’s coauthors, said in the release. “They reflect future spending increases that will no longer occur. Our analysis makes that distinction clear.”

H.R. 1’s multi-year scale down in state-directed payment rates is set to begin in 2028, and are expected to slash some hard-hit states’ Medicaid spending by up to a quarter of their total annual Medicaid spending, per one analysis. Hospitals have broadly criticized the policy changes while spending much of this year adjusting their operations to contend with the future limits supplemental revenue, expectations of increased uncompensated care costs and year-to-year expense pressures like wage and supply inflation.

Additionally, CMS earlier this year proposed state directed payment caps substantially more extensive than what was outlined in H.R. 1 and used as the basis for Monday’s ASPE report. That proposal would extend rate limits beyond the bill’s four outlined service areas (inpatient hospital services, outpatient hospital services, nursing facility services and qualified practitioner services at an academic medical center) to “all [state-directed payments]s for all services in all states.” Hospital groups have flatly opposed the proposal, which more than triples the 10-year estimated savings.

Lawmakers last year, when seeking votes, blunted H.R. 1's funding reductions for rural hospitals by inserting a five-year, $50 billion rural health fund into the legislation. Those funds have come with usage restrictions for states and providers, with critics also noting transparency concerns and the fund's relatively small scale compared to the bill's total projected impact on hospitals.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/regulatory/unpacking-implications-cms-latest-proposed-medicare-advantage-changes" hreflang="en">Unpacking the implications from CMS' latest proposed Medicare Advantage changes</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
Unpacking the implications from CMS' latest proposed Medicare Advantage changes" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Late last week, the Trump administration released a proposed rule that includes a slew of technical changes in Medicare Advantage for the 2028 plan, citing enhanced competition as a key goal.

Analysts dove into the beefy regulation, and project that changes to MA's star ratings program would likely cull more than $100 billion in federal spending over the next decade.

Per a note from Leerink Partners, estimates from the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services suggest that the changes would cut federal spending by $11.5 billion in 2031 and $107 billion through 2037.

The Leerink analysts estimate that about 58% of Medicare Advantage contracts would see lower scores using the newly proposed methodology, with about 20% of contracts losing quality bonus payments, which are tied to star ratings performance.

In the rule, CMS proposed moving to a set of 15 measures to determine the star ratings scores, which are focused on member experience and outcomes. The proposed rule would also eliminate the Reward Factor, and in tandem, these changes "would remove a cushion for contracts sitting at the critical QBP threshold, while concentrating performance across fewer measures," according to the Leerink report.

The analysts said that the potential impact of these proposals "reminds us just how many plans have been living at 4 stars for years with the hugely important Reward Factor."

The report estimates that the changes to the star ratings, if finalized, would have an uneven impact on insurers. For example, Humana would see the number of contracts earning four or more stars decline from 95% to 82.9%. Clover Health, meanwhile, would likely maintain 100% of its contracts earning at least four stars.

UnitedHealthcare, by comparison, could see a drop from having 67.9% of its contracts in plans with at least four stars in 2027 to just 8.8% under the new methodology. For Aetna, the Leerink analysts project a decrease from 70% to 0%.

Given the scale of the changes, the Leerink analysts write that the rule sets the stage for a significant overhaul of the policies and rules that govern Medicare Advantage. The proposal is "almost overwhelming in size, scope and the impact on the industry."

Molly Turco, a policy analyst at TD Cowen, wrote in a note Monday that the proposed rule "is more significant than expected, in both revenue impact and scope and touches most core aspects of MA."

"It appears CMS aimed to reform long-standing concerns, especially Stars, in a way that takes money out of the program, while also loosening regulation on things like agents/brokers, seamless enrollment, network adequacy, etc. to help the program grow," Turco wrote. "However, we think these growth/deregulation policies are unlikely to offset the headwinds for MA."

She noted that while most of the most painful changes wouldn't impact plans until 2031 at the earliest, the delay does suggest that CMS may be comfortable with including more significant short-term changes to risk adjustment in the forthcoming 2028 Advance Notice, which is expected in January.

Ryan Langston, an equity research analyst at TD Cowen, wrote in the note that the proposed rule would likely be net negative for insurers with large, multi-state contracts and more people enrolled with plans earning at least four stars.

The delayed timeline of at least four years does grant plans time to adapt, Langston wrote, but the financial impacts would likely force benefit changes. The proposed rule could also spook investors concerned about what's included in the 2028 rate notice, he said.

"The combination of a smaller measure set, elimination of reward factors and reduced improvement measure protection likely lower Star ratings across the industry and force plans to mitigate lost revenue via benefit revisions," Langston wrote.

The Leerink analysts wrote that health plans are almost certain to fight the regulation. Lawsuits have become commonplace in the stars space, with multiple payers suing the feds over the most recent round of scores.

"We believe a lot of what has been proposed will change and/or not be implemented at all," they wrote. "Expect industry litigation."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/florida-sues-leapfrog-group-misrepresenting-its-hospital-safety-grades-consumers" hreflang="en">Florida sues The Leapfrog Group for 'misrepresenting' its hospital safety grades to consumers</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →
Florida sues The Leapfrog Group for 'misrepresenting' its hospital safety grades to consumers" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Florida’s attorney general has filed a lawsuit against hospital safety watchdog The Leapfrog Group alleging that it misled consumers about how it determines the grades assigned to hospitals, particularly those that declined to voluntarily submit data to the group.

The complaint, which was filed late last week and lists President and CEO Leah Binder alongside her organization, follows a similar suit brought by Tenet hospitals in the state that convinced a judge that nonparticipating hospitals were being “unfairly” penalized in Leapfrog’s methodology.

The group was ordered to withhold safety grades for those hospitals and in grade releases since has voluntarily chosen not to award ratings to hundreds of others that did not submit recent quality data to its surveys. Leapfrog appealed that decision in September, and has said it is reviewing a potential methodology change for upcoming gradings to incorporate non-submitting hospitals.

The Florida Office of Attorney General James Uthmeier’s lawsuit cites that case. It argues that Leapfrog’s poor scores for “at least seven Florida hospitals” misled patients, and outlined specific cases in which patients had declined care over the perceived poor performances of those hospitals.

“The Leapfrog Group told Florida patients its hospital grades were peer-reviewed and backed by experts. That was a lie,” Uthmeier said in a Friday release announcing the litigation. “When hospitals declined to fill out its survey, Leapfrog handed them the worst scores in the country without a shred of data, and patients refused surgery because of it. Leapfrog and its CEO should expect to be held accountable.”

The office requested that the court permanently bar Leapfrog from “misrepresenting to Florida consumers … the data, sources, methods or expert approvals underlying any hospital grade or score they publish,” or from representing the scores as peer reviewed. It also seeks to limit Leapfrog’s solicitation of charitable contributions, make corrective disclosures, and pay out other relief to providers, patients or other affected individuals and groups.

Leapfrog, in a statement to Fierce Healthcare, said the office’s case “poses the specter of outright government censorship,” and was “piggybacking” on the Tenet hospitals’ case “with old allegations Leapfrog strongly refuted.”

“Leapfrog looks forward to answering in court and defending our First Amendment rights and most importantly, patient safety,” the group’s statement reads. “Too many patients are harmed or killed by medical errors and infections in hospitals, and Leapfrog will always stand up for their lives as we have for 26 years.”

The methodology change at the heart of the cases came ahead of Leapfrog’s fall 2024 safety grade publication. It more heavily weighed “imputed” scores for four of Leapfrog’s 32 safety measures that were assigned when recent safety data for the two preceding years are unavailable. The Tenet hospitals, in their case, highlighted internal communications and other testimony that questioned the validity of the changes. Both lawsuits suggest that the changes were made to maximize hospital participation in Leapfrog’s survey, which would bring downstream increases to revenues it collects from allowing hospitals to advertise their grades.

Uthmeier’s office has also filed a friend of the court brief to the appellate court now considering Leapfrog’s appeal, where it similarly argues that Leapfrog’s conduct is a violation of Florida’s consumer protection laws and not protected by the First Amendment.

The new complaint comes the same week as Leapfrog’s latest hospital safety grade release, which similar to the spring release withheld grades for 447 hospitals.

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/providers/provider-groups-file-lawsuit-against-hhs-over-new-ryan-white-funding-rules" hreflang="en">Court strikes down anti-trans Ryan White funding rules 'based in animus, loathing and discrimination'</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →
Court strikes down anti-trans Ryan White funding rules 'based in animus, loathing and discrimination'" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Medical associations and providers notched a legal victory over the federal government in a court ruling vacating federal funding requirements for Ryan White clinics involving gender-affirming care.

Those restrictions would have required recipients of Ryan White HIV/AIDS Program funds to not acknowledge the identities of transgender patients or use funds for gender-affirming care.

It was challenged by the HIV Medicine Association of the Infectious Diseases Society of America (HIVMA), the American Academy of HIV Medicine and the International Association of Providers of AIDS Care in June (see that coverage below), who argued that the policy was contrary to Congress' intent when establishing the program under law in 1991, lacked sufficient explanation and discriminated against transgender people in violation of the First and Fifth Amendments.

The Nov. 19 decision from Judge William G. Young, of the U.S. District Court for the District of Massachusetts, prohibits the government from opposing its conditions in regard to all federal funding and permanently blocks any similar policies from being implemented by associational plaintifs' members, "which covers virtually every HIV care provider in the United States," said Lambda Legal, which acted as co-counsel for the plaintiffs.

"The ruling is a decisive and important victory for transgender people with HIV and protects the ability of physicians and other health care professionals to offer evidence-based care without political interference," Philip Bolduc, M.D., chair of HIVMA, said in a statement reacting to the decision.

Young's opinion and order opened with an acknowledgement that there are "sincere and deeply felt views" regarding transgender identity, and that the issue at question was of legal regulation rather than "the propriety of individual gender expression."

Still, the judge concluded his opinion by writing that the administration's conditions "are based in animus, loathing and discrimination. They do not stand for justice—in fact, they defy it. They are contrary to the laws of this nation and cannot continue to stand."

A group of medical associations and providers has filed a lawsuit challenging new federal funding requirements for Ryan White clinics involving gender-affirming care.

The new requirements, issued in a recent HRSA Notice of Funding Opportunity, require recipients of the Ryan White HIV/AIDS Program funds to not acknowledge the identities of transgender patients or use funds for gender-affirming care. The restrictions undermine the very intent of the program, the plaintiffs say, and violate anti-discrimination components of the Fifth Amendment.

“The Ryan White Program was created as a public health intervention to address the needs of low-income, vulnerable populations who otherwise would not have access to life-saving care,” Melanie Thompson, M.D., co-chair of a primary care guidance panel for the HIV Medicine Association, said in a press release. “It is crucial that Ryan White clinicians be allowed to continue to deliver the most scientifically appropriate treatments for all patients, including transgender individuals, without discrimination if we are to improve health outcomes for all and end the U.S. HIV epidemic."

The lawsuit alleges (PDF) that HHS and HRSA violated the Administrative Procedure Act by exceeding their statutory authority and imposing restrictions that conflict with the Ryan White statute. The plaintiffs ask the court to block enforcement of the funding restrictions.

The case was filed by Lambda Legal and HSF Kramer on behalf of the American Academy of HIV Medicine, the HIV Medicine Association and the International Association of Providers of AIDS Care, plus two providers.

A preliminary hearing will likely be scheduled in a few weeks. Lambda Legal is “cautiously optimistic” about the future of the case, José Abrigo, senior attorney and HIV project director at Lambda Legal, told reporters on a webinar on Thursday afternoon. He called the administration’s actions “unlawful,” and said gender-affirming care in this context includes all types of care, including primary care and mental healthcare.

Specifically, the HRSA Notice of Funding Opportunity reads: “Discretionary awards shall not be used to fund, promote, encourage, subsidize, or facilitate … denial by the recipient of the sex binary in humans, or the belief that sex is a chosen or mutable characteristic.”

Some providers are already preemptively complying with the restrictions, Abrigo said, particularly in Tennessee and Kentucky. It is unclear how many other providers will also stop gender-affirming care. In certain cases, it’s up to individual states or departments of health to enforce the restrictions. It’s possible that medical records could be audited and reviewed for indications of pronouns, per Thompson. “It does matter what someone’s gender and pronouns are listed as,” Thompson said on the webinar, explaining that medical records are shared between providers and help ensure affirming care.

If a clinic is found to be non-compliant, the repercussions are steep, Thompson added. “The penalty for being found out is severe, and puts an entire program’s funding in jeopardy,” she said on the webinar.

Forty percent of American Academy of HIV Medicine members rely on Ryan White funding, of which 40% voluntarily report providing gender-affirming medical care. For low-income trans individuals with HIV, there aren’t many alternative options, per Abrigo. They may seek care through private insurance or potentially through Medicaid, but typically the population relies on Ryan White clinics for services.

Gender-affirming care restrictions under the Trump administration have been commonplace. Over 40 hospitals have reportedly stopped some gender-affirming care services for minors in response to pressure. Last week, the Cleveland Clinic promised the Department of Justice (DOJ) to commit $2 million to detransitioning services and a “decades-long” agreement not to perform gender-affirming care on minors. Delivering most of these services is currently illegal for minors under Ohio state law. Last month, DOJ struck a similar deal with Texas Children’s.

For months, experts have been sounding the alarm about the U.S. HIV response slipping into dangerous instability. In April, the International Association of Providers of AIDS Care (IAPAC) released a data brief finding that 61% of providers have reported at least one HIV service disruption and 68% have seen operational changes such as staffing reductions, service cutbacks and program closures. This was attributed to a combination of policy decisions, funding cuts and social determinants like loss of insurance coverage.

“It’s a catastrophe in the making," José M. Zúñiga, AIPAC's president and CEO, told reporters on the webinar. "It was already happening. This adds fuel."

<a href="https://www.fiercehealthcare.com/payers/wtw-global-health-insurance-costs-set-rise-102-next-year" hreflang="en">WTW: Global health insurance costs set to rise 10.2% next year</a>

📅 2026-11-23  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →
WTW: Global health insurance costs set to rise 10.2% next year" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Global health insurance costs are set to rise by double digits in 2027 for the second straight year, according to a new survey.

WTW released its latest Global Medical Trends Survey, which estimates that the average gross cost for medical benefits will increase by 10.2% in 2027. This follows a 10.1% increase in 2026 and a 9.8% increase in 2025, according to the report.

In the United States, the cost for medical benefits is expected to rise by 10.3% on average, on par with 2026 and up from a 9.4% increase in 2025. Across regions, the highest increases are expected in the Asia Pacific, averaging 12.6% in 2027.

The next-highest region was North America, with a projected 10.5% average increase.

The survey was conducted in June and August 2026, and includes responses from 393 insurers offering coverage across 92 countries. Just under half (49%) of those surveyed said they anticipate elevated medical and pharmacy costs to impact them over the next three years, and likely persist beyond that window.

The WTW analysts said this likely means that insurers will remain focused on cost containment and sharing measures in the near future.

"In the US, healthcare costs remain under sustained pressure from new medical technologies, specialty pharmaceuticals, and the rising use of GLP-1 therapies, alongside higher provider costs and the ongoing demands of chronic and complex conditions," said Courtney Stubblefield, strategy and specialty leader in Health & Benefits at WTW. in an announcement.

More than three quarters (76%) of the surveyed insurers named new medical technologies as the leading cost drive for both medical and pharmaceutical costs. Sixty-five percent cited provider fee increases, and 45% named pharmaceutical advancements.

Cancer was the leading condition driving costs, the survey found, with 62% naming it as the top cost condition. Sixty-one percent of those surveyed said they have seen an increase in cancer diagnoses among individuals under age 40 when compared to the past two years.

However, when looking at the conditions where costs are growing the most quickly, cardiovascular disease rises to No. 1, cited by 54% of the surveyed insurers. Cancer was second on this ranking, and named by 51%, while diabetes was third at 28%.

"To manage this, employers need a multiyear, multifaceted strategy that addresses pharmacy spending and high-cost conditions—such as cancer, cardiovascular disease and diabetes—while keeping care affordable and accessible for employees," Stubblefield said.

"pervert glasses" named word of the year by Australian national dictionary

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Each year, the Macquarie Dictionary calls together a select Committee to discuss the new words and definitions that have entered the Macquarie Dictionary over the past year. The aim of this is to select one of these to be awarded the Committee’s Choice Word of the Year.

As part of this process, our editors create a longlist of words across 13 categories which, over the course of a few hours, are whittled down into a shortlist. And from this, the Word of the Year is chosen.

The Committee this year was comprised of:

The shortlist is then opened to the public to register what you think the Word of the Year should be. The word with the most votes is awarded the People’s Choice Word of the Year.

You can review the final shortlist here (and for those interested, the longlist here). Thank you for helping us decide on the defining new word of 2026!

2026 has undeniably been another year of rapid technological development, especially in the world of AI, as it finds its way into more and more parts of our lives. This year’s Word of the Year winner and runners up reflect this continued development, but also recognise that life does continue in and around our changing world.

plural noun Colloquial camera glasses; smart glasses. [from the use by some of filming or taking photos of others without their knowledge or consent]

‘The coinage pervert glasses is a succinct reflection of the state of the world in 2026 in so many ways. On the one hand, we’ve seen a massive acceleration in the capabilities of AI and products that integrate it. At the same time, the undeniably critical nickname pervert glasses mirrors the widespread calls to hit the brakes on this seemingly unchecked development. The name represents the year we’ve all endured, with AI, the manosphere, increasing surveillance in supermarkets, and more. Sure these new gadgets might be marketed as smart glasses or camera glasses, but it seems to us that pervert glasses paints a more apt reflection of the year.’

noun (polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome) a disorder, possibly genetic, leading to irregularities in ovulation and menstruation in women; associated with acne, excess hair growth, insulin resistance, and obesity. Formerly known as PCOS (polycystic ovarian syndrome).

‘As awareness of medical misogyny (a WOTY 2025 honourable mention) spreads, we hope to see more terms like PMOS entering the dictionary. It shows the power that language can hold, both negatively in the problems caused by the commonly misleading term PCOS, and positively in how this decade-long Australian-led campaign to rename this condition was recently adopted worldwide.’

adjective (especially of artificial intelligence) able to act or make decisions independently.

‘While it might not be the most flashy, funny, or socially impactful word on the surface, agentic has seen an exponential increase in usage over the last 12 months. Where 2024 and 2025 saw the development and massive spread of large language models, the release of agentic AI tools such as OpenClaw and the mischief they recently wrought has brought this otherwise very technical term firmly into the mainstream.’

noun Colloquial a temporary halt in conversation by people in the inner-west suburbs of Sydney, to await the passing of a loud, low-flying aircraft, after which the conversation continues. [so called because the suburb Marrickville lies directly beneath a flight path, in very close proximity to the international airport]

‘It is undeniable that we are facing major cultural shifts in the digital world, and as a result, seeing huge language innovation at an international scale. Marrickville pause represents a departure from this trend – a highly localised term for a purely physical phenomenon, but one that is so universally experienced by people who spend time in the area.’

And we have a threepeat! For the third year running, the public have aligned with the Committee’s choice by selecting pervert glasses to be crowned People’s Choice Word of the Year 2026!

plural noun Colloquial camera glasses; smart glasses. [from the use by some of filming or taking photos of others without their knowledge or consent]

noun a supposed condition characterised by short-term memory loss, attention deficit, impaired decision-making, etc., thought to result from an excessive reliance on digital devices and artificial intelligence.

‘Digital dementia is, of course, not a real medical diagnosis but that hasn’t stopped the fear of an increasing reliance on digital devices from resonating deeply. People are finding that they’re struggling to navigate without Google Maps and to make and remember plans without iCal. It will be interesting to see if all of this outsourcing does in fact lead to impaired cognition in the long term.’

interjection Colloquial a term used by a reader to indicate to others that an online item has been created by AI and is not worth reading. [AI + d(on’t) or d(idn’t) r(ead); modelled on TL;DR]

‘A nice play on TL;DR (a code for too long; didn’t read), AI;DR gives fellow readers an instant heads-up that the text to follow is what they consider to be AI slop. As the sheer volume of AI generated text is ever-increasing, this succinct code will become a much-welcomed time saver.’

Innovaccer integrates Grok Voice into Gravity Voice AI agents" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Autonomous artificial intelligence healthcare operations platform Innovaccer is expanding its Gravity Voice AI capabilities with a new integration of Grok Voice.

Through the integration, the platform’s healthcare intelligence data, workflow infrastructure and voice AI will be combined with Grok Voice at scale. Trained on real phone audio, Grok Voice is HIPAA-eligible and is available in more than 25 languages.

CEO and co-founder Abhinav Shashank said in a statement healthcare voice AI is moving from "answering questions to doing work."

"We scaled from hundreds of thousands of interactions to more than five million and showed measurable results in appointments, referrals, and engagement," Shashank said. Specifically, Innovaccer says patient voice interactions have grown from roughly 250,000 to more than five million since its launch six months ago. And, it has tripled referral intakes, helped organizations book "significantly more" appointments and reduce documentation times by 90% per case.

Founded in 2014, the company builds software solutions to unify enterprise data and applies AI to automate manual tasks and streamline workflows for payers and providers. As of May, it says it serves more than 200 health systems and payers, 80 million patients across the U.S. and 95% of community pharmacies.

The Grok integration is now live for customers.

The platform plans to integrate other Grok models via API in the future, executives say, including Grok Build and Grok Bot.

The top goalscorers for club & country in 2026 so far: Kane, Yamal, Mbappe, Raphinha…

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Big names from Real Madrid, Barcelona, Arsenal and Bayern Munich feature alongside some surprise names in the most prolific goalscorers of the calendar year so far.

Some big names are surprisingly absent from the most prolific goalscorers of 2026 to date.

Drawn from Europe’s five major leagues, here are the top 10 goalscorers in 2026 across club and international competitions.

Schick has had reason to celebrate in 2026. Earlier this year, he reached the milestone of 100 goals for Bayer Leverkusen.

He’s been on fire this season, with 13 goals from 15 games for the German side.

There won’t be any more international goals from Schick, though. He retired from Czech Republic duty after the World Cup.

The jury is still out on Gyokeres, who remains a perplexing forward.

This calendar year has seen him look borderline anonymous and increasingly sidelined at Arsenal.

And yet Gyokeres also fired Sweden to the World Cup with a hat-trick against Ukraine and a late winner against Poland. He’s basically the Ikea Gerd Muller at this point.

A surprise entry, perhaps, given his struggles for Real Madrid.

The Brazilian has scored just one La Liga goal so far this season, and his goalscoring has been downright ordinary for Los Blancos for quite some time now.

He’s scored 18 goals in all competitions for his club this calendar year, averaging out a not-great average of one every 186 minutes. His Brazil team-mate Raphinha, by contrast, has averaged a goal every 70 minutes for Barcelona in 2026.

But his international exploits look considerably more impressive, while his five goals in this international break – two against Japan, a hat-trick against Singapore – come after five goals at the World Cup. He’s scored 11 goals in 14 appearances for the Selecao in 2026.

Vini’s place in Jose Mourinho’s favoured XI has come under threat from summer signing Yan Diomande. Watch this space.

A fairly recent entrant into the top 10 after heading into the international break on the back of a hat trick against Union Berlin.

Olise was the Bundesliga’s best assister last season but has been more than capable of finding the net himself a fair few times too.

He didn’t score any goals at the World Cup but netted the winner in Zinedine Zidane’s second game in charge of France. The best Fantasy Footballer around right now.

What a year it’s been for Malen. Started it as a benchwarmer at Aston Villa. Set to finish it as one of Europe’s most lethal strikers at Roma.

Malen made an immediate impact after his January move to the Italian capital and his scoring rate has barely slowed down since.

For some reason, his form hasn’t quite translated into international football; he hasn’t scored for the Netherlands at all this year.

But high-flying Roma are laughing. Malen almost won the Serie A golden boot last season despite arriving midway through and he’s a leading contender for the gong this season.

Just the four goals at the weekend against Parma, too.

Yamal has long been talked up as a Ballon d’Or contender, and slowly but surely he’s getting the numbers to back it up.

The teenager’s goalscoring output has grown season on season, and 2025-26 was no exception. February’s hat-trick against Villarreal was evidence of a player about to go truly stratospheric.

Unfortunately, the final furlong of the campaign was hit by a hamstring injury, or else we suspect he’d be a few places higher up in this list.

He needed just 10 minutes in his first World Cup start to find the back of the net, although that would be his only goal in Spain’s triumph out in North America.

Now he’s looking fit and hungry – at least based on the evidence of his exceptional form of late.

The Brazilian has an exceptional tally of 33 goals for Barcelona so far this calendar year (plus one for Brazil), which is especially impressive when you consider he’s missed quite a lot of action due to injury.

He averages a goal every 49 minutes for Barca this season – to put that in context, Kylian Mbappe averages a goal every 84 minutes for Real Madrid.

Raphinha has scored in all but four of the Barca games he’s played in in 2026-27, scoring hat tricks in two, and currently leads the Europe-wide goalscoring charts with 15 in the league.

The top three are the last three winners of the European Golden Shoe. This list has taken a while to take shape, but the frontrunners are the usual suspects.

Haaland’s four goals against Iraq and Senegal in the group stages finally saw him climb up into the top 10, from which he was surprisingly absent in the latter half of the 2025-26 club season, before he added to his World Cup tally against the Ivory Coast. Then there were two goals against Brazil.

He finally drew a blank as Norway exited the World Cup at the hands of England at the quarter-final stage. He earned a well-deserved rest before spearheading Man City’s new era under Enzo Maresca.

Same as it ever was with nine goals in nine Premier League appearances for City this season, plus braces against Porto and PSG and another Champions League goal against his favourite opponents, RB Leipzig.

Like his rival Haaland, it somehow took Mbappe until mid-June to enter the top 10.

Mbappe kicked off the year in superb style, scoring in six successive starts for Real Madrid (nine goals in total) before dipping considerably with just one goal in his next seven appearances.

The World Cup and his start to 2026-27 show that this is a player you’d be daft to ever question, though.

Arguably the finest player in world football right now. How often have you been able to say that about an Englishman?

The only player in Europe’s five major leagues to average a better-than-goal-a-game output so far this calendar year, the England captain has 41 goals in 40 appearances for Bayern Munich in all competitions in 2026 – including home and away against Real Madrid and PSG.

He capped off his superb season with a hat-trick in the DFB Pokal final. Two goals against Croatia showed Kane carried his form into the World Cup, where he eventually scored six times.

Unbelievably, the Norway game was Kane’s first knockout appearance for club and country in 2025-26 in which he didn’t score. Which, of course, he followed with a blank against Argentina.

Surprisingly enough, Kane failed to add to his tally by drawing a blank in his first three Bayern appearances of the 2026-27 campaign.

But it’s back to normal service with his Bayern tally standing at 10 goals in 14 games. The Czechs and Croatia have been on the receiving end in the Nations League too.

Lionel Messi’s calendar year record is safe after Kane’s injury could see him sidelined until 2027. We’d still expect him to remain top of this list come New Year’s Eve, but it’s not impossible that he ends up overtaken.

By 2040, 51% of People Living with HIV Could Be Over 50

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Health  |  Read Source →

Follow Healthline on our socials for all things health.

Advances in antiretroviral therapy (ART) have helped people living with HIV live longer, contributing to a growing population of older adults with the virus.

In fact, a recent report published in The Lancet HIV projects that approximately 51% of people living with HIV worldwide will be over age 50 by 2040. An estimated 96% of these older adults will live in low- and middle-income countries.

The report’s authors emphasized the “pressing need” for HIV prevention and screening services tailored to older adults, who face additional age-related challenges when accessing care.

These challenges include stigma related to both age and HIV, a higher burden of chronic health conditions, taking multiple medications, delayed diagnoses, physical limitations, and increased financial and social vulnerability.

Factors like these “influence HIV risk for older adults in complex and dynamic ways,” according to the report authors.

Several factors have contributed to the growing population of older adults living with HIV, including:

Many adults over age 50 who are living with HIV acquired the virus during young adulthood in the late 1990s and early 2000s. During that period, some experienced prolonged periods of uncontrolled HIV and exposure to older ART medications with more severe side effects, which may have contributed to long-term inflammation and other health complications.

William Schaffner, MD, professor in the Division of Infectious Diseases at Vanderbilt University, who wasn’t involved in the report, told Healthline, “That older persons with HIV are on track to outnumber younger persons with HIV infection is a double-barreled success story.”

“First, the effective treatment of persons with HIV infection has resulted in the expectation of normal or near-normal lifespans; thus, more older persons living with HIV infection,” he said. “This is a great success; both in creating effective, easy-to-use medicines as well as developing medical care systems that can reach such patients and provide lifetime care.”

Schaffner said the second success has been educating younger people about HIV prevention, helping reduce new infections.

Older adults also account for a growing proportion of new HIV diagnoses. They are more likely to receive a diagnosis later in the course of infection, which can delay treatment and increase the risk of serious health complications and death.

Mathematical models project that the average age of people living with HIV worldwide will increase from 41 in 2025 to 50 by 2040, largely driven by demographic shifts in low- and middle-income countries.

While the World Health Organization (WHO) does not specify an upper age limit for HIV testing in its published overview, U.S. guidelines focus on specific age groups.

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) recommends routine HIV testing for people ages 13 to 64, and the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force recommends testing for people ages 15 to 65.

However, these age limits don’t mean older adults shouldn’t be tested for HIV, as both U.S. organizations also recommend testing older adults who may be at risk.

Older adults can still be sexually active and have a chance of acquiring HIV. Certain physical changes that come with age, such as thinning of vaginal tissue, may also increase the risk of HIV infection.

Despite these risks, older adults are often overlooked in HIV prevention campaigns, research, and clinical trials. The report’s authors say more needs to be done to address these gaps.

“Targeted and tailored HIV prevention and screening services for older adults are a pressing global need,” the report authors stated.

Schaffner agreed with this and added, “Increasing the effectiveness of diagnosis and treatment of HIV infection in older adults will increase their expectation of a healthier, longer life.”

The report recommends several approaches to improving HIV prevention and screening among older adults, including:

While Schaffner noted that HIV treatment has “advanced spectacularly over the past 20 years,” making it possible for people living with HIV to lead longer, healthier lives, access to these treatments remains a challenge in many parts of the world.

11th Circuit tosses ex-Georgia Tech professor’s lawsuit against university auditors

📅 Nov 23, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The 11th Circuit panel found that the former Georgia Tech professor's racketeering charges stemmed from an independent investigation by state authorities, not a university audit.

(CN) â An 11th Circuit panel rejected a former Georgia Tech professorâs suit against two university auditors he blamed for his arrest in a racketeering case.

Chief U.S. Circuit Judge William Pryor wrote in a unanimous opinion issued Monday that Joy Laskar failed to establish that the auditors, Phillip Hurd and Patrick Jenkins, conducted the investigation that led to his prosecution. Laskarâs charges stemmed from an independent investigation conducted by state authorities â not the university officials’ internal audit or their report of suspected misconduct, the panel found.

âBefore obtaining an arrest warrant, Agent Vorrasi and McLaughlin conducted an independent investigation,â the George W. Bush appointee wrote in the opinion. âThey reviewed the same documents reviewed during the audit. They spent four months analyzing thousands of documents, deposing Sayana employees and consulting other university officials.â

Laskar was director of the Georgia Electronic Design Center, a research center where tech companies collaborate with researchers after paying membership dues. He was also co-founder and president of Sayana, a company that designed and developed high-frequency microchips.

In 2009, Georgia Tech began investigating a $600,000 shortfall at the center, according to court filings. Hurd and Jenkins learned during their audit that Sayana had billed the design center more than $1 million for the manufacture of prototype microchips which were later sent to a South Korean institute, which paid for the prototypes and research.

Laskar failed to disclose in conflict-of-interest reports filed with the university between 2007 and 2010 that he had a 42.5% stake in Sayana, according to the auditors. Sayana also did not pay membership fees to use the centerâs resources.

The auditors reported the suspected malfeasance to the university, which in turn sent the report to the Georgia Department of Law.

Laskar was charged in 2010 with two counts of racketeering, but a Superior Court judge dismissed the case four years later after finding the statute of limitations barred the charges.

Laskar filed a civil rights action against the auditors in 2018 in the Northern District of Georgia, claiming the government employees intentionally provided false and misleading information to law enforcement.

U.S. District Judge William M. Ray, a Donald Trump appointee, initially dismissed the case because Laskar did not receive a âfavorable terminationâ in criminal court, or a finding of innocence, which could maintain a malicious prosecution suit. The decision was reversed on appeal, but Ray dismissed it again last year after finding Laskar failed to support his claims that the auditors operated as law enforcement officers.

The 11th Circuit panel appeared skeptical of Laskarâs arguments during a hearing this month.

U.S. Circuit Judge Embry Kidd, a Joe Biden appointee, suggested the auditorsâ recommendation was no different from any other tip to law enforcement.

âSometimes investigations rely heavily on information provided by others,â he said. âThat does not mean that the decision by the investigator wasnât independent of the people providing the information.â

Pryor said it did not matter if the auditors provided advice and information to state authorities. The investigators specifically stated that their warrant application was based on their independent investigation and their own assessment of the evidence.

Pryor filed a separate concurring opinion Monday addressing the U.S. Supreme Courtâs decision in Thompson v. Clark, which determined a plaintiff only needs to show that a prosecution ended without a conviction to establish the “favorable termination” element for a malicious prosecution case.

Pryor defended the use of the common-law tort of malicious prosecution as the appropriate analogy for constitutional claims involving seizures pursuant to legal process, such as arrests based on warrants.

He distinguished those claims from challenges to warrantless arrests, explaining that the two types of seizures involve different legal considerations.

U.S. Circuit Judge Barbara Lagoa, a Trump appointee, rounded out the panel.
