Its lead shrinks to 3.83x on the 15 workloads without AI tuning.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Architect Labs, a Palo Alto startup, said in a research blog post that its AI systems discovered, designed, and verified Frostbite, a general-purpose CPU with a new instruction set “built from the ground up.” Across 25 workloads, a four-tile Frostbite cluster delivered 4.59 times the performance per watt of a similarly sized “silicon-proven” dual-issue, in-order CPU core. Those numbers come from a simulated 7nm design that has not yet been made in silicon, and the company says it can’t name the comparison core for legal reasons.

Conventional CPUs have hardware that “rediscovers, at runtime, which instructions actually depend on each other.” Multicore designs also need deep cache hierarchies and coherence protocols, which keep every core’s view of shared memory consistent, but scale poorly and cost area and power. Architect Labs’ alternative is what it calls “multithreading for free,” where the program itself exposes the parallel work, without threads, locks, or out-of-order hardware required for that discovery.

Frostbite’s programs are compiled to interaction nets, a model of computation from 1990 in which a program is a graph of nodes and computation happens by rewriting pairs of connected nodes, and the chip is designed around this distinct paradigm. Pairs of nodes being rewritten never overlap, so they can run in any order, or all at once, and arrive at the same result. Every value is also used exactly once, so data can stay in small local memories instead of large shared caches.

An example of the difference given by the team is a 5x5 chess-like board with five queens, placed so none can attack another. A typical program searches for solutions one board at a time. As an interaction net, the same search breaks into 3,183 small operations, and the longest chain that has to run in order is only 262 steps, so on average about 12 operations are ready to run at once at each step. Nothing about the program was rewritten to get there, as the parallel work was already intrinsic.

Each of the chip’s tiles, small identical processing units linked by an on-chip network, has its own small local memory. Four tiles, plus four L3 nodes, form the cluster that’s benchmarked against the conventional core for the performance claims, but the full layout has 16 tiles. The design reached 800 MHz in early synthesis but ended up at 500 MHz in the final layout. The cluster also averaged 8.77 times the comparison core’s performance per area, and the table below shows how its performance per watt varies by workload.

Frostbite performance per watt vs. comparison core

It may come as some surprise that the AI which built Frostbite started with what the company calls “zero architectural priors,” though the team first picked the workloads it had to do well on, and this sort of blank slatism may have its advantages in avoiding bias. Instead, the agents read papers and benchmarks, built Python models, and then selected one concept for focus. The original 141-page PDF output, which contained a programming model, runtime, and high-level blocks, was deleted after the first register-transfer level (RTL) design was generated, because “many of the numbers and estimates were well off what was physically possible.”

That PDF was only ever a starting point. Once the agents picked an architecture, they moved away from English — as it can’t get every detail right or catch every mistake, according to the team — and wrote the spec in the company’s own language, called ASL. ASL translates to Lean, a proof checker, so it can be machine-checked, which the company says stops a large class of bugs and cuts down on agents gaming their objectives. From this, the agents devised Arcmodel, a software model of the whole chip written in ASL, before any circuit design existed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Simulating the RTL is slow, so the agents tested ideas on the model instead, which ran about 200 times faster. At first, the model’s power estimates were off by up to almost 250%, but an automated loop adjusted it until it matched the circuit design within just 5%.

From there, swarms of agents proposed changes in parallel, and each one was checked against the spec, tried in the fast model, built into the circuit design, and measured for power before being kept or dropped. This led to 58 ideas tried, with 37 kept. Separately, a behavior emerged that “was never specified by any human,” in what is known as work stealing: a tile with nothing to do can pull spare work from a neighbor or, in lieu of anything to do, go idle to save power.

Share of energy (one four-tile cluster, mean of 20 workloads, excluding clock distribution)

Fetching, decoding, and issuing instructions

Clock distribution (share of total energy, left out of the rows above)

This isn’t Architect Labs’ first design, as it showed off Redwood, an AI inference accelerator for robots and drones, in August. In that case, the company says its AI generated the RTL, verification, firmware, drivers, and kernels in under two weeks from a spec written by two human architects. Redwood still runs only on an FPGA and hasn’t been made in silicon, though the company says it is working toward having it built at TSMC.

Tendrils Compute, a possible rival based in Cambridge, U.K., raised funding earlier this month for a processor it says will have “thousands of cores operating in parallel.” It is also developing Vine, the same language Frostbite’s programs are written in.

Architect Labs says more benchmarks are coming, but they’re only part of the plan. It also wants an FPGA-hosted “software playground for anybody to run a program on Frostbite” along with a “preliminary operating system.” Eventually, it plans to release Frostbite’s RTL and instruction set.

In the meantime, the company plans to cover more workloads and compare “a broader set of CPUs,” including larger Frostbite mesh configurations against multicore versions of the comparison core with large caches. It also plans to test against “larger, wider, out-of-order CPUs,” with these comparisons due in the coming months on popular CPU benchmark suites such as SPEC and PARSEC.

Let the Deals Pick Your Destination – Start With What’s Cheap From Your Airport

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Many or all of the credit card, banking, and other offers on this site are from companies that pay us. For example, we may be paid when you click a link, are approved for a product, or open an account. Compensation may influence which products we cover and how, where, and in what order they appear. It doesn’t influence our editorial evaluations, ratings, or opinions, which are ours alone. We don’t include every bank, credit card company, or offer available in the marketplace.

Pick your vacation spot first, and you pay whatever it costs to fly there. Instead, search a fare map from your home airport with the destination left blank. Set price alerts on a short list of trips you’d happily take, and book when one drops to a price you’re willing to pay.

Say a couple wants Paris in June, and round-trip economy tickets from their home airport cost $1,030 each. When an alert flags Lisbon in May at $480 round trip, two tickets there cost $1,100 less than two to Paris.

A fare map shows you swaps like that before you’ve picked a place. On Google Flights, open Explore, enter your home airport, leave the destination blank, and choose flexible dates, such as a one-week trip in the next six months. The map then pins a round-trip price on cities near and far, so you can pick from the ones that are cheap from your airport right now.

If you’re set on one city, save by searching flexible dates and nearby airports for that one flight, or by going in its shoulder season, the weeks just before and after its peak season.

Next, let the deals come to you. Pick three to five trips you’d happily take, search each on Google Flights, turn on Track prices, and choose Any dates. Google then emails you when a route’s lowest price drops significantly over a month, and otherwise sends a regular email with its best fares.

For trips you didn’t think to watch, add Going, a deal newsletter whose free plan emails a sample of economy deals from your home airport to the continental U.S. Its $49-a-year Premium plan adds international deals. Join the email lists of the airlines that fly from your airport, too, to hear about their fare sales.

Keep a trip fund and a few open weeks on your calendar, so you can say yes to a deal right away. Book the hotel at a refundable rate too, so a cheaper option that turns up later can still replace it.

Write down the most you’d pay for each trip on your list, and book the day an alert comes in under it.

Start your short list of trips on Google Flights.

Stephen King's Carrie Inspired This Iconic 90s Rock Album

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Few images in horror are as recognizable as pig blood raining down on freshly-crowned prom queen Carrie White. It's an image first born from "Carrie" author Stephen King's imagination, and it's been brought to life repeatedly on-screen, starting with Brian De Palma's 1976 "Carrie" film adaptation on up to Mike Flanagan's 2026 "Carrie" TV series.

Carrie's bloody prom night is such a famous scene that its cultural influence extends beyond movies. The rock band Hole's second, and best, album, 1994's "Live Through This," features cover art of a prom queen holding flowers close to her face with a wide-eyed smile but tear-drenched makeup. Featuring model Leilani Bishop and photographed by Ellen von Unwerth, the cover represented Hole's inflammatory frontwoman Courtney Love's attempt to recreate the pivotal scene from De Palma's "Carrie."

"We were on the phone for one hour. I didn't say much but listened, and Courtney had the idea of reenacting the scene of the movie 'Carrie,' which I loved, too," as von Unwerth once told fashion magazine AnOther. "Live Through This" earned much critical lauding and commercial success in its day and is still ranked as one of the best albums of 1994, if not in alternative rock history. The aggressive songs on it both cut deep and flat out rock.

Even the "Carrie"-inspired album cover art is famous in its own right. Rolling Stone has ranked "Live Through This" as the 13th best album cover ever. Later female musicians like Olivia Rodrigo have staged "Live Through This" homages featuring themselves, much like how the original album cover paid homage to "Carrie." That homage isn't skin deep, either. The "Carrie" allusions on the album's cover reflect the album's themes of how the world likes to (metaphorically) drop pig's blood on women, especially ones who step into the spotlight.

The title "Live Through This" immediately suggests someone going through a tough time and, barely, finding the strength to, well, live through it. As Courtney Love has explained, the title comes from a line of dialogue in a different movie — "Gone with the Wind" — and was largely inspired by the microscope the media had put her life under. (Love's husband, Kurt Cobain, died by suicide in April 1994, about a week before the release of "Live Through This.")

Allusions to violence against the feminine runs through the album. "Asking for It" is titled after a common rape victim blaming refrain. (Love was inspired by how, while once crowdsurfing at a show, the crowd tore her clothes off, a pretty terrifying mix of sexual assault and objectifying fandom.) "Jennifer's Body" (the namesake of the cult classic horror film) is about a woman being murdered, with the chorus alluding to how people later "found pieces of Jennifer's body." "Plump" and "Doll Parts" explore the body image issues with which people's unforgiving gaze infects women.

"Softer, Softest" features the verse "burn the witch, just bring me back her head," referencing the historical persecution and execution of women as "witches." Besides the album cover, that's the line that most directly connects "Live Through This" to "Carrie," a story all about religious trauma. The withdrawn and telekinetic Carrie White is the kind of woman who'd be burned as a witch in darker ages.

Carrie's pain is often explicitly feminine, too, as the story infamously kicks off with her getting her first period. The allusions to "Carrie" in "Live Through This" infuse subtext that, with all that the world makes women live through, it's miraculous that more of them don't explode like Carrie White did.

Like so many things with AI, it’s not that large language models opened up some wholly new capability. For as long as there have been gadgets, a few users have been hacking, modding, jailbreaking, and tweaking them to their liking. What is new is that it’s easy now. People all over the world are discovering that they can use large language models to hack their hardware simply by prompting Claude Code or Codex to do it. It has led to a boom in developer-friendly gadgets and created a market for better software on just about every device you can think of.

For one developer in India who goes by Gathin T, the whole thing started almost on a whim. “One afternoon, I was fiddling with my TV, and it was just really shitty,” he tells me. His was a set made by Xiaomi and running Android TV, with so many ads and so much interface chaos it was hard to even change inputs. And he got to thinking: If you can install another launcher on your Android phone, maybe you can do the same to your TV. “The first thing that came to mind was to remove the bloatware from the TV,” he says. “Then I thought, why not make it look better?”

Gathin prompted Claude Code and discovered that with a couple of permissions tweaks and a toggle of USB debugging mode, his chatbot could control his TV as long as they were on the same Wi-Fi network. An hour and a half later, Gathin had redesigned his TV to his liking — no more onscreen ads, a simpler interface, easier access to his PlayStation on another input. He posted on X about what he’d done and promptly went viral. So Gathin put together his work, bought a domain, and launched CleanYourTV as a tool for helping other people solve their own television interfaces. It became one of the most popular repositories on GitHub, got thousands of pull requests and feature ideas, and inspired countless people to point their own chatbot at their big screen.

Gathin is careful to note that the only TVs that work this easily are based on Android. (He has Claude Code-d his way through other operating systems, but those involve more explicit jailbreaking and rooting, so he’s more reluctant to share the process so widely.) But for vibe hackers everywhere, the good news is that Android kind of runs everything. From dishwashers to digital photo frames to luxury cars, even gadgets that don’t look like Android often run Android. Even when they don’t, odds are good that there’s another way in. If there’s a hidden API, your LLM can find it. If the firmware file lives on a website somewhere, your LLM can unpack it, explore it, and repackage it to your liking.

Again, few of the technical capabilities here are new, and enterprising developers have been doing this kind of work for many years. Some have even been using AI for several years to make the work easier. But when Claude Code first had its “oh my gosh it works” moment in late 2025, developers began leaning on the tools even more — and non-developers began discovering they, too, could build software now. And over the course of 2026, more and more people began pointing that capability at their hardware.

Now, it seems, all hardware is hacker hardware. Camera enthusiasts embraced the possibilities of vibe hacking as fast as anyone, using LLMs to repackage their camera software in all sorts of ways. Firmware hacks like Magic Lantern are a staple of the camera community, but suddenly it seems like everyone is using AI to add new recording capabilities, support higher resolutions, or even add new looks and color profiles to their shots. Many are using the tools to give their old gear new life; other, more enterprising vibe coders are pointing their bots at their brand-new hardware.

The music world is being similarly upended. Budding musicians are turning cheap synths into powerful new kinds of gear, and a cheap gadget known as the M-Vave FM-1 has become a popular target for custom firmware and new features. M-Vave has even released an open-source firmware, embracing the hackers as other companies have tried to keep them out. The FM-1 is cheap, it’s easy to hack, and it’s raising questions about what higher-end manufacturers can even do to compete.

Gamers, who are also using AI to decompile and mod the games they love, are using LLMs to add new features to their consoles. One vibe coder found a way to play PlayStation 2 discs on a PlayStation 5; lots of gamers have built scripts that allow them to use their favorite controllers on other devices.

In the games world in particular, this has caused the industry to fight back. Game makers are looking for ways to protect their work from being decompiled and made available for free and on other platforms. Luke Dicken, a longtime gaming executive who was recently Take-Two’s head of artificial intelligence, recently predicted to Kotakuthat the industry will start to embrace “more invasive DRM, platform-level solutions, and probably more of a push towards streaming solutions since nobody can decompile what they can’t get their hands on.” Hardware makers will push harder to lock down their systems; companies across the board will pursue legal action to protect their intellectual property.

All this hardware access also creates new risks for users. When you install a vibe-coded version of your camera’s firmware, you are now well outside the bounds of what the camera manufacturer is equipped to help you solve. You might be stuck asking Claude to fix the problems Claude created. As Ritchie Roesch wrote recently at Fuji X Weekly, “It’s not so much if someone’s camera will become a permanent paperweight, it’s a matter of when and how many, and whether it will be yours.”

The safer (and less litigious) route for the enterprising vibe hacker lies in an entirely new kind of hardware. ESP32 modules, which package networking chips, microphones, speakers, and more into a simple and hacker-friendly package, are suddenly everywhere in hardware communities. (Think Raspberry Pis, but even cheaper and more versatile.) So sure, you can use Claude Code to jailbreak and de-Amazon your Kindle, but why do that when you can buy a device like the M5Stack PaperMono, an ESP32-based e-paper device you can make into whatever you want?

ESP32 modules are perfectly suited to help you build the kind of hyper-specific device Apple or Samsung would never consider. With a few dollars and a few prompts, you can build a way to see real-time sea conditions, rig up a monitor for your whole DJ system, or just play Metal Gear Solidon the go. You can even have a dedicated device for your family’s shopping list.

The arms race between device manufacturers and vibe hackers is only going to continue. As AI models get more powerful, and both the tools and their users get more sophisticated, it’ll be easier and easier for anyone to point their bot at their TV, their car, or their washing machine and see what happens. Some manufacturers will try their best to keep the hackers out; others will look for ways to safely embrace them. Some users will find incredible new functionality for their tech; others will find they’ve created extraordinarily large and expensive paperweights.

Before long, we’re going to see a lot of ESP32 devices turned into more consumer-friendly hardware. Meta is already leading the charge with its Muse Charm and the Muse Gadgets program, through which you can either buy a nicely packaged ESP32 module or use your own to quickly spin up a dedicated AI device. This kind of gadget — just a simple computing module in a pretty box — is going to be everywhere.

At the same time, we’re already seeing some vibe hackers turn into proper software vendors. Gathin, for instance, has spent a lot of time reckoning with the responsibility he feels for what happens when people use CleanYourTV to hack their televisions. He’s tweaked the prompts he shares to first take a backup of your TV’s software in order to make it easy to restore if something goes wrong, and to make note of every change it makes along the way. The CleanYourTV system also checks its progress as it goes and spins up agents to manually test if something has gone wrong, and Gathin hopes it’s increasingly foolproof.

But it’s not foolproof. It might never be. Devices change all the time; every software update introduces new possibilities and new problems. So Gathin recommends the same thing I hear from vibe coders and vibe hackers all the time: Never give the bots access to something you can’t afford to lose. “Even for fiddling with stuff,” he says, “I have an external computer which runs through Linux. So I just use it like a sandbox environment to test things out.” If you’re going to try and vibe-hack your phone (which lots of people are doing) or your computer (which lots of people are also doing), do it carefully. And make lots of backups.

Your questions about Apple HomeView’s audio, answered

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

The smart display sounds good and works well as a touchscreen music controller, but CarPlay apps are messy.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

After I published my review of the HomeView, Apple’s first smart display, y’all still had a ton of questions about how it works as a smart speaker. So, I spent a bunch more time testing its audio capabilities and support for third-party music apps — and found a few more frustrations along the way. Here are the answers to all your burning questions. Have more? I’ll answer them in the comments.

The HomeView isn’t a replacement for a Sonos One, a full-size HomePod, or a similar-size speaker. Those are much better speakers. In size and sound, the HomeView is closer to the HomePod Mini. I tested it alongside the Mini 2 (which sounds marginally better than the first-gen Mini) and found the tabletop HomeView mostly comparable, with these caveats: The HomeView has better bass and is slightly louder, but it’s not as clear and is less room-filling.

You can hear them both in action in this video, alongside the full-size HomePod, the Sonos One, and the wall-mounted HomeView:

The difference comes down to the hardware. The Mini 2 has a single full-range driver and two passive radiators, whereas the tabletop HomeView has two drivers in the display, plus a woofer and two passive radiators in its base. While the woofer adds some oomph, the HomeView’s display, which sits over the speaker base, partly covers it, affecting the sound compared to the Mini. Apple added an accelerometer to the display to account for changing the angle. Still, the Mini, which doesn’t sound as loud, fills a room better, also aided by its 360-degree sound. (The HomeView does support Spatial Audio with Dolby Atmos, which the Mini 2 doesn’t.)

The wall-mounted HomeView has only two drivers, with computational audio Apple says is “tuned specifically for on-wall placement.” It sounds fine but is noticeably thinner than the tabletop version, and bass is basically non-existent. I wouldn’t recommend it for its sound quality, but it’s great for talking to a visitor through your video doorbell.

No. While you can group HomeViews with any other AirPlay speakers to play music out of them all at once, you can’t pair two for a stereo setup or pair a HomeView with a HomePod or Mini. I like the sound of two Minis paired in stereo and use this setup in my office and my bedroom. They also work as speakers for my Apple TVs in those rooms.

If you have $300 to spend on a good-sounding smart speaker setup, go with two Minis over a HomeView. The HomeView’s real benefit is the screen. I cover its uses extensively in my full review, but for audio in particular, I like that the screen acts like a jukebox, making it easy to change songs, select new music, and scroll through your podcasts or audiobooks — without needing to find a quarter! With a HomePod, you rely on Siri voice control or your Apple device to select content.

You can AirPlay to the HomeView as with any other AirPlay speaker, and audio handoff works the same as it does on HomePods. Tap your phone to the HomeView, and whatever is playing transfers to the speakers and the screen. Handoff also works in reverse, letting you send any content back to your phone. One addition with the HomeView: You can now hand off video.

In testing, handoff worked for Apple TV, Apple Music, Apple Podcasts, Spotify, Pocket Casts, YouTube Music, FaceTime, and phone calls — although phone calls sometimes dropped. YouTube and YouTube TV videos also handed off, even though they don’t have an app on the HomeView. But Netflix and Amazon Prime Video only played audio, though both played video in the Safari browser.

One word of caution: Handoff is aggressive. As with HomePods, if you’re holding your phone within a few inches, the HomeView will try to hand off content.

HomeView doesn’t let you set a default speaker to permanently route audio to instead of using the built-in speaker. However, you can play audio to any AirPlay speaker or group of speakers. I tested this on HomePods, Sonos speakers, and a Samsung TV, and it worked smoothly.

For native apps (Apple Music, Podcasts, and Safari), the HomeView remembers the last speaker or speaker pair you used and automatically plays content through those when you return. If it’s been a while since you opened the app, it pops up the option to choose the last-used speakers.

Spotify has no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving — only I’m not driving; I’m in my kitchen!

Things get messier with third-party audio apps such as Spotify and Audible, and with Apple apps that use the CarPlay interface, such as Apple News and Apple TV. The HomeView only automatically uses the last speakers if you choose content from the HomeView screen. If you use Siri voice control, it defaults to the HomeView speaker.

So, “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan from Spotify” played music from HomeView’s speaker and not the stereo pair of Minis in my office I’d last been listening to Spotify on. I could direct it to play music there by adding “on Office,” but that’s more of a mouthful than just “Hey Siri, play ‘Femininomenon’ by Chappell Roan,” which is all you need to say if you use Apple Music.

Which brings me to another one of your most-asked questions — and my biggest complaint about this device as a smart speaker: How does it support third-party audio apps? Like second-class citizens.

Spotify works on the HomeView, but with limitations that apply to all non-native and third-party apps: they only show up when your phone is connected to the HomeView via your profile, so only you can use them.

Apple has taken a shortcut here: Rather than building a dedicated HomeView experience for app developers, the HomeView uses Apple’s existing CarPlay platform. This makes it easier for developers to put their apps on the HomeView — if an audio or video app supports CarPlay, it already works on the HomeView (there’s no support for other types of CarPlay apps).

In practice, this creates frustrating limitations, including no search function, requiring a user profile on the HomeView, and your phone on the same Wi-Fi network. It also results in slower performance and a laggy, inconsistent experience compared to the native apps.

I tried several apps: Pocket Casts, Amazon Music, YouTube Music, Spotify, and Audible. They all appeared in my HomeView app drawer, and I could access them, choose content via streamlined interfaces, and then play audio on the HomeView or AirPlay from it to any speakers.

But CarPlay apps don’t work as well as the built-in audio apps — Music, Podcasts, and Safari. They take longer to load and respond more slowly. You also can’t type a search query in any of them, as you can in the native apps, because they don’t have a search field or keyboard. Instead, you have to scroll through content to find what you want or use Siri voice search.

Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”

There’s no search box because you’re not supposed to type while driving; only I’m not driving — I’m in my kitchen! This shows how much of a CarPlay port the apps are; they haven’t been updated to account for the fact that this device is stationary and in your home. Case in point, when I first pulled up the Apple TV app (the only CarPlay video app currently supported), a warning popped up telling me to make sure I was stationary before watching any video.

Playing audio through CarPlay apps is also buggy. It didn’t always bring up the music control screen, so I had to use my iPhone to control it. Plus, the Audible app often refused to load at all, and didn’t work with Siri voice search. Me: “Siri, resume my Audible book.” Siri: “Shuffling latest songs from Apple Music.”

CarPlay support feels like a stopgap, and Apple needs to work with developers to refine the software and add HomeView-specific controls for a better, more integrated experience.

Much of the confusion around the HomeView stems from treating it like a smart speaker when it’s really a smart display. I get it; I’ve spent the last decade frustrated by smart displays from Google and Amazon that promise a lot but rarely do it all well.

If you don’t care about the other features of a smart display and just want a smart speaker, then the reason to buy a HomeView over a HomePod is if you have a use for it as a touchscreen music controller. This lets anyone in the household easily browse and control what’s playing. Bear in mind, though, Apple Music (with a subscription) is the only app anyone can use to play music on the HomeView, as CarPlay apps require a user profile.

If you want a smart speaker primarily to play music and are happy using your voice or phone to control it, go for a HomePod. If you use Spotify or other third-party music services, consider an Echo Dot or Google Home smart speaker; both let you set a default speaker and a default music provider — such as Spotify.

If you already own HomePods and use Apple Music, the HomeView’s touchscreen will fit in nicely as a simple way to control audio. Just don’t buy one expecting it to fully replace a dedicated smart speaker.

Lazio 0-1 Milan Primavera: Rossoneri into play-off spots with fourth straight win

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

Milan Primavera have begun to build some real momentum, as they recorded another win over Lazio in Sunday’s early kick-off.

Before the November international break, the Primavera managed to build on their mid-week cup win with a strong performance against Monza, winning 5-0 away from home. Max Anastasio, Mattia Zaramella (x2), Riccardo Grassini and Yanis Messaoudi scored to move the Rossoneri up to 11th in the standings.

Read SempreMilan ad-free and get access to exclusive news. Click here for a free trial!

That made it three wins in a row, and today it became four in the capital against Lazio. Giovanni Renna’s side scored the only goal of the game in a 1-0 victory that they were made to fight hard for.

The Rossoneri took the lead after just eight minutes when right-back Luca Nolli registered his fifth assist of the season with a perfect cross that found Mattia Angelicchio in the box, who deceived the Biancocelesti defence and headed past D’Alessio.

The home side had a great chance to equalise in the 21st minute, but Gatto squandered it, failing to tap in in front of goal. In the second half, Milan managed to contain the Biancocelesti as they pushed to find an equaliser. They had some chances, but the Diavolo held firm.

With another three points on the board, Renna’s boys moved up to fourth place in the table, while awaiting the result of Bologna’s game against Torino tomorrow.

Australia: Use Vietnam Dialogue to Press for Rights Reforms

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: World  |  Read Source →

Click to expand Image Vietnam's President To Lam leads his delegation in a meeting with Philippine President Ferdinand Marcos Jr. at Malacanang Palace in Manila, Philippines, June 1, 2026. © 2026 Rolex Dela Pena/AP Photo

(Sydney) – Australian officials should press the Vietnamese government to release political prisoners and reverse its intensifying repression of human rights during the upcoming 21st Australia-Vietnam Human Rights Dialogue, Human Rights Watch said today. The case of an Australian national Tran Hiep, a democracy and human rights activist, is of particular concern. He has been detained in Vietnam since he was reported missing in Cambodia in September 2026. 

Human Rights Watch urged Australia, in a submission to the Department of Foreign Affairs and Trade, to seek clear, concrete, and measurable progress from Vietnam on issues including political prisoners, labor rights, transnational repression, and freedom of religion and belief.

“Since Australia’s last dialogue, Vietnam has sharply intensified human rights abuses both inside and outside the country,” said Daniela Gavshon, Australia director at Human Rights Watch. “Australia needs to make it clear to Vietnam that merely holding another meeting is not itself a measure of progress.”

Vietnamese authorities have stepped up transnational repression, reaching beyond the country’s borders to suppress dissent by targeting human rights activists, government critics, and others in violation of their human rights. In late September, the overseas political organization Viet Tan alleged that Vietnamese police had detained three of its members in Cambodia, including Tran Hiep, and transferred them to Vietnam.

The Vietnamese government currently holds more than 160 political prisoners for exercising their rights to free expression or for peacefully advocating human rights and democracy. Dozens more are detained and awaiting trial. During the first nine months of 2026, Vietnamese courts convicted and sentenced at least 31 activists to lengthy prison terms, including the prominent bloggers Hoang Thi Hong Thai and Huynh Ngoc Tuan. During this period, the police arrested at least 46 other people for criticizing the government.

The upcoming dialogue should also focus on Vietnam’s suppression of independent labor organizing. Both Australia and Vietnam are parties to the Comprehensive and Progressive Agreement for Trans-Pacific Partnership, which contains provisions obligating Vietnam to carry out reforms and to allow the creation of independent trade unions. However, as Human Rights Watch recently documented, the Vietnamese government has not actually reformed its policies and practices, and prevents workers from forming and operating genuinely independent unions.

The Australian government should communicate concerns about these violations. It should also outline how Vietnam’s failures to reform will affect Australia-Vietnam relations and implementation of trade and tariff provisions in the partnership. 

“The Australia-Vietnam human rights dialogue will accomplish little so long as concrete outcomes aren’t formalized,” Gavshon said. “A human rights dialogue shouldn’t be a box-ticking exercise but instead a diplomatic exchange with real-world implications.”

The U.S. Let Volvo Keep Selling Cars. Polestar Got A No. What About Mercedes?

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Browse this and many other websites without banner ads, personalized tracking and video ads.

The Volvo EX-90 and Polestar 3 roll off the same assembly line in South Carolina. They share much of their hardware and, according to Polestar, run the same software. However, when each manufacturer asked Washington for permission to keep selling them in America, they got different answers.

Now Congress is considering a tougher law aimed at keeping Chinese cars out of the U.S., one that could reintroduce headaches for Volvo and put another major automaker in a tough position. Congress is close to a deal on legislation that would ban cars from automakers with more than 15% Chinese ownership stake, as Bloomberg reported. This would be a tough pill for Mercedes-Benz, as around 20% of the company's stock is owned by Chinese investors.

But the publication reported that Republican and Democratic lawmakers are nearing a deal that could spare Mercedes. They want to send a bill to the president by the end of the year, but its terms have not yet been finalized.

Volvo EX90 assembly at the Volvo Ridgeville factory in South Carolina

The Volvo and Polestar decisions came under a Commerce Department rule finalized in 2025 aimed at controlling connectivity and self-driving technology. Washington’s concern is that a foreign adversary could use those systems to collect data on Americans or even remotely control vehicles. The new restrictions on certain Chinese- or Russian-linked software take effect for the 2027 model year. Hardware restrictions will follow in 2030 and apply to both gas and electric cars, including ones assembled on U.S. soil.

Chinese giant Geely has a majority stake in Volvo Cars, so the Swedish automaker had to apply for specific authorization to keep importing and selling connected cars. The Department of Commerce granted it. Volvo told InsideEVs it still has to meet the same software requirements as every other automaker. The Swedish foreign trade minister confirmed his government worked closely with Volvo to achieve compliance.

Polestar is another Sweden-based automaker that is controlled by Geely. It applied and was denied authorization to keep selling cars from model year 2027 onward. So why was Polestar axed despite Volvo being saved? Polestar told its dealers it had asked the Department of Commerce that very question and had yet to get an explanation, pointing to the common software and the shared South Carolina production line.

Even though Polestar is a Volvo spinoff and also has a Swedish connection, the country’s trade minister said Polestar didn’t want the same assistance as Volvo. That’s one difference we know between their applications, but the U.S. government hasn’t explicitly said why things shook out this way, and Polestar did not appeal the decision.

Mercedes-Benz EQS SUV production in Alabama

The proposed Connected Vehicle Security Act would write existing restrictions into federal law and extend the U.S. sales ban to automakers with more than 15% Chinese ownership, even if they build their cars in America.

China’s state-owned BAIC Group owns 9.98% of Mercedes-Benz. Geely founder Li Shufu holds another 9.69% through an investment company. Taken together, that’s just shy of 20%, and over the new proposed threshold.

For reference, Geely owns 79% of Volvo and Geely founder Li Shufu reported beneficial ownership of 60.5% of Polestar’s shares in a September 2026 SEC filing.

That doesn't mean Mercedes will be locked out of the market, though, even if the bill passes as currently written.

“The U.S. is an extremely important market for us, and we're not going to do anything to jeopardize our success in the past and our success in the future,” Jason Hoff, CEO of Mercedes-Benz North America, said during a media roundtable with InsideEVs last week.

When asked whether the company had a plan for its Chinese shareholders to divest, Hoff declined to comment on specific conversations Mercedes has with shareholders. He was more direct about the legislation itself.

“You know, we always have and always will support anything that protects the national security of the United States. So we've never opposed this bill, but you know, in the end, we'll also take whatever steps we need to comply with it if it does get passed," Hoff said.

Mercedes says its Chinese shareholders have no seats on its management board and no say in day-to-day decisions. They could still have voting rights as shareholders. That matters because, according to Bloomberg, lawmakers are considering an exception that would let companies exclude passive investments with no voting rights from the proposed 15% ownership calculation.

It’s still unclear how Mercedes would prove its Chinese stakes qualify. In the meantime, House members have asked Mercedes to provide documents, answer two dozen questions by December 1, and arrange a meeting with its U.S. boss.

Texas college football rankings: Red Raiders and Longhorns rule the state

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Texas Tech likely will have to beat TCU on Thanksgiving night and then Utah in the Big 12 title game to finally get some respect in the College Football Playoff rankings.

The Red Raiders are doing just fine in our rankings, where Tech is still No. 1 over Texas.

Tech's only loss was to 6-5 Cincinnati on the road. The Longhorns have two losses to 7-4 Oklahoma in Dallas and to 7-4 Florida in Austin.

The Chronicle's rankings for the 13 FBS teams in Texas (with a special nod to Tarleton State, which finished 12-0 in FCS play):

The Red Raiders have consecutive 10-win seasons for first time in school history. The latest was a 20-10 win over Baylor. "You know, we're not perfect," coach Joey McGuire said. "We're a team that fights extremely hard … we find a way to win."

Arch Manning went through Texas' senior day festivities then played like the QB everyone expected all along in a 45-3 win over Arkansas. The only question was if it was his last home game. Texas could host a CFP playoff game unless A&M finds a way to ruin the Longhorns' season.

The Roadrunners may not end up as the highest-rated team from the Group of 6 conferences, but they are best in the American after hanging on for a 19-17 win over UAB.

Hello, Keisean Henderson. The future is bright for Cougars after freshman starts in place of injured Conner Weigman and leads 34-31 win over West Virginia with a drive for winning field goal as time expired.

The Bobcats have won five of their last six after beating Washington State 42-14 but were knocked out of the Pac-12 title game when San Diego State upset Boise State.

The Mustangs are winless against ranked teams after a 24-6 loss at No. 6 Notre Dame. At least it was much closer than last week's loss to Wake Forest.

The Horned Frogs had a few moments against No. 2 Utah before falling 38-17. Texas Tech is next and then a decision on future of coach Sonny Dykes.

The Bears are 2-2 since firing Dave Aranda. After Saturday's 20-10 loss to Texas Tech, Baylor wraps ups its regular season at UH. Then it's on to a new coach. Jeff Traylor? G.J. Kinne? Zach Kittley or RG3?

Coach Neal Brown said the Mean Green's loss to Tulane was a "travesty." We have to agree.

All will be forgotten if the Aggies are able to beat Texas on Friday. Well, maybe not all. The offense was just as inept (Mike Elko's word from last week) with Brady Hart at QB in a 28-7 loss to Oklahoma.

Rice can throw the ball, too. Patrick Crayton Jr.'s pass and Jacurri Brown's catch were a thing of beauty for a 30-yard touchdown with six seconds left to beat Temple 31-27. Rice, which broke a nine-game winning streak, finishes at home against Army.

Scotty Walden, who was so pumped a few years ago after a game against Texas, was let go on Monday. The Miners were no match for Air Force in a 52-3 loss. One more game.

The Bearkats made a game of it in a 31-28 loss to Middle Tennessee State. It was the second 3-point loss of the season for Sam, which will be hiring a new coach.

Randy Flores brings a former player's perspective to Houston Astros GM job - and knows its limits

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

In his first year as St. Louis Cardinals scouting director, Randy Flores was watching a pitcher who threw a four-seam fastball and a curveball. The mix can be the backbone of a north-south pitching profile. But to Flores, a former major-league reliever, it looked suboptimal.

"I'm like, 'No, no, no - you sink it and slide it,'" said Flores, reciting an approach he gleaned from former Cardinals pitching coach Dave Duncan.

As a slight left-hander who did not throw especially hard, Flores carved out a playing career that lasted parts of eight major-league seasons. So, his methods had merit. But as an executive now, he recalls the scouting anecdote as something of a cautionary tale.

"Being a former player, if you're anchored too much to, 'This is how it was done when I was playing,' it's the wrong thing," Flores said.

Exploring other perspectives and means and finding a balance has been part of Flores' evolution as an executive, one that led to his hiring this month as general manager of the Houston Astros. It is also an arc to which only a handful of the majors' current highest-ranking baseball executives can relate.

Of the current presidents of baseball operations, Jerry Dipoto (Mariners), Chris Young (Rangers), Craig Breslow (Red Sox) and Buster Posey (Giants) played in the majors. So did general managers Chris Getz (White Sox) and Flores, whom the Astros hired after 11-plus seasons in St. Louis' front office.

That many top executives did not play in the majors, or at all professionally, illustrates how it is far from required. It might even invite the question of how such experience informs former players' approach to the role, if at all, as Flores settles into running a baseball operations department for the first time.

"You probably have more empathy … for what a player goes through, from the moment they sign their first professional contract to when they get to the big leagues," Dipoto said at last week's GM meetings in Las Vegas.

Dipoto and Breslow were relievers in the majors, like Flores. Dipoto posited that background could help "to understand the life of the guy who (is) not always the most thought-of player on the roster."

"Outside of that, I think it's just business as usual," Dipoto said. "We each went through our own, I guess, apprenticeship (after playing).

"You take the lessons you learn and apply them, just like any other general manager does when they get their first job. We just maybe have that slight - I don't even want to call it an advantage - that slight difference in that we have lived life in these players' shoes and probably as a result have a little bit more empathy rather than just frustration. It's a hard game."

Mariners GM Jerry Dipoto, a former major league reliever, is among a half-dozen ex-players who head up baseball operations departments. He said that experience includes empathy for players' journeys through pro ball. (Lindsey Wasson/AP)

After his playing career, Dipoto held roles in scouting before becoming general manager of the Angels in 2011 and for the Mariners in 2015. Breslow spent five seasons in the Cubs' front office, working heavily in pitching development, before the Red Sox hired him to lead their baseball operations.

"My hope would be it adds a level of credibility to the role and a level of empathy," Breslow said, "given that there really isn't anything I ask player to do that I haven't kind of been through myself, having been traded, promoted, released, started games, come in from the pen.

"And I think understanding the mindset of a player and how they're likely to respond and react to different suggestions and requests, I'm hopeful (that) is helpful in terms of understanding how to approach certain conversations."

Flores, 51, spent several years out of professional baseball after pitching his final season in the minors in 2011. He returned to the University of Southern California, his alma mater, to pursue a master's degree with a self-described goal to "chart a career path combining business and sports." He started a company that captured and provided amateur game video for use by recruiters and scouts.

In 2015, the Cardinals sought a scouting director to replace Chris Correa, who was dismissed and later imprisoned for illegally accessing the Astros' computer database. John Mozeliak, then St. Louis' GM, recalled that the Cardinals did not want to overhaul their draft process, which was producing results. Flores, who played five seasons in St. Louis, interviewed and landed the job, his first in a front office.

"There's more to leading a department than just believing in a model and running with it," Mozeliak said this month. "But I think his interpersonal skills are outstanding. I think he has the ability to walk into any type of room and be able to speak to the group."

New Astros GM Randy Flores (left), pictured with former Cardinals teammate Matt Morris, pitched in the majors for parts of eight seasons before moving into front-office work with St. Louis. (MARK HUMPHREY/AP)

Flores oversaw St. Louis' amateur drafts from 2016-26, including for the past eight seasons as an assistant general manager. In recent years, Flores gained experience in other areas of the Cardinals' operations including pro scouting and salary arbitration, he said at his introductory press conference in Houston.

"He's definitely ambitious," said Mozeliak, who left St. Louis after the 2025 season and is now the Angels' interim general manager. "And there was a certain point where he started to ask to get more exposure to different elements of baseball operations. So as he was having early success on the scouting side, it was just natural to keep letting him evolve and giving him exposure to more of the things that you really see under the hood when you're working with or as a general manager."

Chaim Bloom joined the Cardinals as an advisor in early 2024 and succeeded Mozeliak as their president of baseball operations. In their two seasons overlapping in St. Louis, Bloom said he found Flores "gifted in how he can navigate and understand any type of person or group of people you come across in this game" and appreciated "the way he challenged me to think differently about some things."

"It's hard to go into specific detail on that," Bloom said. "But just in terms of some of the discussions we would have on players or how to play the draft - and (also) some things in the pro space, just thinking about different acquisitions - the way he's able to assimilate information, hear different voices and kind of distill it down into something that's actionable, I found that really helpful."

For executives with playing backgrounds, the exchange of viewpoints is a two-way street. It's incumbent on former players to consider how their experience might limit how they view situations, said Brandon Gomes, who spent five seasons as a reliever with the Rays and is now the Dodgers' general manager under president of baseball operations Andrew Friedman.

"Obviously, we have a lot of fantastic executives that never played that have other really helpful perspectives without a bias of having played," Gomes said. "I think you just have to be aware of it; we all have biases. So just being aware it could be there and being a good listener, understanding that you may be too far anchored in that, is a good way to go about it."

Changes within the game can require it. Flores noted during his Astros introduction how access to the kind of data and technology available today might have impacted his own playing career and said he considers analytics "the fabric (and) foundation of … evidence-based decision-making" as an executive.

While data use is prevalent in front offices, though, those who can weigh it against firsthand experience playing in major-league games are less so.

"(Flores) is someone that anchored to these modern tools to help with the decision-making," Mozeliak said. "But he also has great feel for what happens inside a clubhouse or on the field. So being able to pair those two skill sets, I think, will just allow him to make better decisions along the way."

The Astros wagered as much in hiring Flores as general manager, handing him a major-league roster that finished 81-81 in 2026 and a farm system rated among the sport's thinnest by outside evaluators. Astros owner Jim Crane, who maintains involvement in baseball operations along with advisor and Hall of Fame first baseman Jeff Bagwell, touted Flores' playing career afterward as part of his "well-rounded" résumé.

Flores said last week that early on as a scouting director, he felt the need to guard against "attaching too much to my bias of my empirical experience" in evaluating players. Still, those years as a pitcher form an inextricable part of the path that led Flores to the role he assumes now in Houston.

"I understand how hard it is to play this game and I think I have some empathy for that," Flores said. "And I do believe the modern tools and curriculum and the technology and coaching that are available help drive player improvements at a pace much faster than I experienced as player via trial and error.

"So my hope is that I understand how difficult that road is, but I also drive our organization to help those players be the best version of themselves."

49ers-Vikings roundtable: Mexico City showdown between two of NFC's best

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

The San Francisco 49ers have already delivered some big wins - the opener in Australia against the Rams and beating the Seahawks in Seattle among them - but suddenly their game in Mexico City against a really good Minnesota Vikings team might be their biggest test yet. Before the two teams tangle, we asked columnist Ann Killion and reporters Eric Branch and Harrison Rich for their answers to some key questions about the matchup.

Harrison Rich: Bosa has not looked like his old self since returning two weeks ago from a calf injury. He didn't look like himself earlier in the season, either, when he was dealing with knee tendinitis coming off his second torn right ACL. The 29-year-old defensive end hasn't recorded a sack in four games this season, his longest NFL drought. He pointed to two factors Wednesday for his struggles: He's still learning some of the quirks of defensive coordinator Raheem Morris' scheme, and he's still knocking off some rust. Bosa said he took "for granted" some of the things he used to be able to do automatically. Sunday's game, expected to have playoff-like intensity, will tell us a lot about the heights Bosa is still capable of reaching.

Ann Killion: It's probably safe to say that Bosa will never again be the dominant force that he was in 2021 and 2022. Four years later, he's 29 and has a history of serious knee injuries. The fact that he thought, as he said this week, that he could "coast" and do the things he used to do "automatically" is an indication of just how hard it is for elite athletes to stay at that level, not only physically but mentally. Bosa's honest assessment this week means he will be even more in the spotlight in Sunday's game, where Minnesota's pass rush is expected to be the star.

Eric Branch: It's a question Bosa might be asking himself. It was notable this week that Bosa admitted he was surprised he hasn't been able to return from last year's torn ACL and "automatically" perform at his All-Pro level. His extensive medical file includes three torn ACLs, two on the right knee he injured in 2025. Still, Bosa has shown some dominant flashes, such as when he hit Dak Prescott's arm in mid-throw last Sunday in Dallas after bullying right tackle Terence Steele with a bull rush. Also, Bosa has played in just four games this season, which qualifies as a small sample size. Verdict: It's premature to bury Bosa.

Rich: The 49ers know what Cousins likes to do. He gets the ball out quickly. They saw that in Week 9. What they don't know, however, is how much more aggressive Cousins might be pushing the ball downfield in this Vikings offense, compared to how he played in a Raiders offense devoid of weapons. He has a solid run game to lean on, and he has an elite wideout in Justin Jefferson. If the 49ers' pass rush struggles continue, and their cornerbacks enter Sunday at less than 100% health, they could get carved up. If they stuff the run and force Cousins out of the pocket some, things could swing in their favor.

Killion: This is a fun and highly unusual situation, to play an elite quarterback twice in three weeks with two different teams. Cousins is a smart quarterback and now has much better offensive players around him, compared to what he had two weeks ago with the Raiders. The 49ers intercepted Cousins' passes three times the last time they met, but as always with the 49ers defense, their effectiveness depends on how healthy they are. Cousins probably learned something from the film of Dak Prescott tearing up the 49ers for 40 points last week.

Branch: Advantage, Cousins. It might not be a significant edge, but Cousins will benefit from having just studied and seen the same defense. Meanwhile, the 49ers will see the same QB. But Cousins will be in a different uniform and offense while operating with a better supporting cast. That supporting cast will include Minnesota's ferocious defense, which should mean Cousins won't be in pass-every-down mode as he was when in his three-interception clunker in a 38-17 loss in Santa Clara with the defensively challenged Raiders on Nov. 8.

The 49ers could be in for a rude awakening, at least to start this game, coming off matchups against the NFL's worst two defenses. The Vikings sacked Packers quarterback Jordan Love eight times last week. Brock Purdy was sacked five times against Dallas, and his head will be spinning against a blitz-heavy defense led by coordinator Brian Flores, who is 5-0 when matched up in some capacity against Kyle Shanahan. How well-prepared Purdy and Shanahan are for Minnesota's initial punch - and eventual counter-punch - will be the key to this matchup.

Killion: This is definitely the biggest test of the season for the 49ers offense. As Harrison points out, Flores is one guy who has seemed to solve Shanahan's offense. The 49ers like to play from ahead, so it's important that they try to penetrate the Vikings defense early. Most troubling for the 49ers is Minnesota's ferocious pass rush, which leads the league with 37 sacks. After keeping Purdy well protected early in the season, he has been sacked 13 times in the past five games, by far less imposing opponents than what he'll see Sunday.

Branch: Not as well as they just did against the inept Cowboys, who stripped defensive coordinator Christian Parker of his play-calling duties after his unit allowed 46 points and 584 yards last Sunday. Unlike Parker, who had pass-rushing great Von Miller in coverage against Christian McCaffrey while everyone else rushed Purdy, Flores and his Vikings crew won't be handing out any gimmes. If the 49ers improve to 9-1, and Shanahan outschemes Flores … do they hand out Coach of the Year awards in November?

Rich: I correctly predicted San Francisco's first loss of the season, and I think I'll correctly predict the second. This isn't me saying the Vikings will be a better team come January, but the 49ers' secondary is quite banged up, the defensive line is still rounding into shape and Sunday will be their ninth consecutive day on the road. Plus, even if recuperating wide receiver Mike Evans plays, it'll be his first game off an extended layoff. Give me Minnesota in a close one. Vikings 24, 49ers 23.

This is pretty much a pick 'em game. I went against my better instincts in Week 1, picking against the 49ers despite being fully convinced that Rams coach Sean McVay's plan for traveling to Australia was completely bananas. I believe the 49ers' dedication to fully preparing their players for all of the conditions they will face is the right approach. Based on that, their week in Colorado Springs, and the Vikings' decision to not try to adapt to Mexico City's lung-crushing altitude in advance, I'll pick the 49ers. 49ers 22, Vikings 17.

Branch: The Vikings are 7-2 despite starting either Kyler Murray or Carson Wentz at QB in their first eight games. Last week, they answered the question of what they could do if they got even semi-competent QB play: Vikings 30, Packers 10. At Lambeau Field. This turn-back-the-clock version of Cousins at 38, coupled with this maniacal version of Flores, who is blitzing at a more than 70% rate, might mean Minnesota has become the NFC's biggest bully. Vikings 23, 49ers 17.

This article originally published at 49ers-Vikings roundtable: Mexico City showdown between two of NFC's best.

Dripping Springs, Legacy Ranch among Austin-area football teams to win area-round games

Nine Austin-area high school football teams across numerous classifications extended their seasons with wins in the area round of the UIL playoffs.

Here are how those squads emerged victorious this week.

Lake Travis Cavaliers wide receiver Luca Antoun (0) celebrates after winning the area championship and advancing in the playoffs after beating Cibolo Steele in overtime at Rattler Stadium in San Marcos, Nov. 20, 2026. (Sara Diggins/Austin American-Statesman)

Running back Teddy Lofgren tossed a 12-yard touchdown to Luca Antoun to set up the conversion. Antoun also returned a kick 92-yards for a touchdown earlier in the game.

PREVIEW: Westlake volleyball faces reigning champions in state final

Round Rock made it past the area round for the first time in a decade with a 42-25 win over previously undefeated San Antonio Johnson in Friday's Class 6A Division I battle. Tyson Cummings ran for 147 yards and three touchdowns, leading a Dragons offense that ran for 317 yards. Quarterback Dov Stickler threw for 207 yards and a score while adding 54 yards and a score on the ground.

Westlake cruised to a 59-14 win over San Antonio Pieper in Class 6A Division II action Thursday. Freshman CJ Jones ran for 114 yards and five touchdowns on just 10 carries. Quarterback Patton Jones completed 9-of-13 passes for 117 yards.

Dripping Springs Tigers wide receiver Dodge Reid (3) celebrates after beating Cedar Ridge 42-35 in the bi-district championship in their first game of the Texas UIL State football playoffs at Tiger Stadium in Dripping Springs, Nov. 13, 2026. (Sara Diggins/Austin American-Statesman)

Dripping Springs scored 28 points in the third quarter to create distance in a 59-28 Class 6A Division II win over Converse Judson. The Tigers had 603 yards of total offense in the game, according to the San Antonio Express-News. Dripping Springs has totaled more than 1,100 yards in its two postseason wins.

Jordan Anderson's 94-yard kickoff return for a touchdown with a minute left put Weiss ahead in its 28-26 win over Port Arthur Memorial on Friday. Cove Darbyshire passed for 442 yards and six touchdowns as Anderson beat Corpus Christi Veterans Memorial 58-29 to advance to the regional semifinals in Class 5A Division I. Darbyshire also ran for 89 yards and a score.

Legacy Ranch continued an impressive first varsity season Friday with a 31-0 win over Corpus Christi Ray in Class 5A Division II.

Wimberley running back Jakari Washington (26) runs the ball during the game against Manor New Tech at Wimberley High School on Friday, Oct. 30, 2026 in Wimberley, Texas. (Aaron E. Martinez/Austin American-Statesman)

Wimberley remained undefeated in dominant fashion Thursday in its 70-6 win over Kingsville King in Class 4A Division II. Sophomore quarterback Jackson Ketter tossed three touchdowns while the running back trio of Jakari Washington, Jobi Hightower-Santos and Bennett German each added two scores on the ground.

Undefeated Thorndale made quick work of Weimar as it won 49-7 in Class 2A Division I Thursday. Quarterback Shiner Thurman threw for 222 yards and four touchdowns in the victory.

This is Thorndale's first time winning two playoff games since 2013.

Personal Consumer Issues • Re: Driveway basketball hoop

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Other  |  Read Source →

My sons are in their 30's so it's been a while. Not sure what an expensive vs less expensive basketball hoop gives you. We had one that had an easily adjustable height and it lasted a long time, I think we got it from Walmart. It did have some rust on it but all will rust.

What I found was after a while they stopped playing with it even though they played basketball on a recreational league. We have a 2 car width driveway so it's not like there wasn't any room for them to practice. Something I did and wish I didn't was to put sand into the base, I saw people put the bags of sand onto the base and not into the base ... I'm sure it made getting rid of the hoop much easier.

Of course everyone's different and your kids may get more use out of a hoop then my kids did.

Liverpool v Man United LIVE: Pochettino names first team as Premier League rivals clash

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Please refresh the page or navigate to another page on the site to be automatically logged inPlease refresh your browser to be logged in

Liverpool host their rivals looking to pile more misery on United after they entered the weekend in the Premier League relegation zone

Want to bookmark your favourite articles and stories to read or reference later? Start your Independent Membership today.

Mauricio Pochettino takes charge of his first Manchester United game and is thrown straight into the deep end as he faces fierce Premier League rivals Liverpool at Anfield.

It’s been a whirlwind week for Pochettino since arriving back in England but the former Tottenham and Chelsea manager has outlined his plan to bring success back to Old Trafford after a turbulent spell and alarming dip of results under Michael Carrick.

United enter the weekend in the relegation zone, with Liverpool having the opportunity to pile more misery on their rivals and continue their progress under Andoni Iraola. The Reds will also be out for revenge after losing both games against United last season, including a 2-1 defeat at Anfield.

No game against Liverpool is a considered a free-hit for a United manager but for Pochettino, ending the club’s nine-match winless run with a first victory away from home this season would be some start.

Follow updates from Liverpool v Man United, below

Back to Fernandes and Pochettino, who has committed to the Portuguese as United’s ‘leader’ as he bids to inspire a high-octane style of football to guide the club out and away from the relegation zone. Jack Rathborn reports:

From one skipper to another: Liverpool captain Virgil van Dijk remains uncertain of whether he will remain at the club beyond the end of the season.

The 35-year-old centre-back is approaching the final six months of his contract, which expires next summer.

He believes that he can keep playing at the top level for “many more years” but says he has “no idea” whether that will be at Anfield as Liverpool’s decision-makers continue to ponder over a fresh offer.

One major thing in Pochettino’s in-tray will be the future of his captain, Bruno Fernandes, who has had an indifferent start to this season - much like his whole team - but has been instrumental in the past.

Fernandes has revealed that discussions over a new contract have stalled because it isn’t “the right moment” to be negotiating.

The Portuguese said an offer had been made in the summer, with his contract due to expire at the end of this season, but negotiations were not ongoing as he insisted on prioritising the club’s return to form.

The good news for Pochettino is that although Liverpool are fifth in the top flight and enjoying a decent spell under Andoni Iraola, they have won just two of their last nine games against United.

And they are winless in their last three clashes between these two rivals at home, while United won both fixtures last term. Liverpool only have one win from five at Anfield this season.

Liverpool host Man United at Anfield today with kick-off at 4.30pm GMT.

Viewers in the UK can watch the game on Sky Sports Main Event and Premier League, with coverage from 4pm.

After a run of eight points from a possible 30 and just 10 games of this campaign, United’s hierarchy opted to part ways with Carrick.

Pochettino is the next man in the hot seat - the eighth permanent manager at Old Trafford in 13 years - and he arrives with United having slipped from 18th to 19th in the Premier League table after yesterday’s results.

A win over arch-rivals Liverpool, at Anfield, on his first day in charge, would be quite the statement...

What is always a blockbuster fixture has had an even more dramatic build-up than usual, with this United’s first Premier League game under new manager Mauricio Pochettino after he replaced the sacked Michael Carrick over the international break.

We’ll have all the latest updates as well as team news and line-ups here.

Spanish Media Cast Trent Alexander-Arnold Verdict After Real Madrid 2-1 Celta Vigo

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Sports  |  Read Source →

Rob is senior football editor at GiveMeSport, with over 15 years experience covering sport in various capacities since graduating from the University of Gloucestershire in 2008 with a degree in journalism. A childhood obsession for all things football developed into a career writing about the beautiful game, initially producing content for clients including Luton Town Football Club and the PGMOL. His first big interviews were with Premier League referees Howard Webb and Michael Oliver in 2009. He then spent four years producing written content for a betting website, mainly focusing on football, and was there to cover various games and tournaments including the 2010 World Cup and Euro 2012. Rob joined GiveMeSport in 2013 (initially as a sub-editor before becoming a senior writer and then senior editor in 2020) and has covered countless football matches and other sporting events, interviewed various high-profile figures including Rio Ferdinand, Peter Crouch and Theo Walcott, and written well over 10,000 articles during that time. Rob has played a key role in GiveMeSport's coverage of the World Cups in 2014, 2018 and 2022, plus Euro 2016, Euro 2020 and Euro 2024, along with many Champions League finals. While football is Rob's area of expertise, he has also covered a wide range of other sports during his time at GiveMeSport including boxing, tennis and darts, as well as the 2016, 2020 and 2024 Olympic Games.

The Spanish media have cast a strong verdict on Trent Alexander-Arnold after the England international’s performance for Real Madrid against Celta Vigo on Saturday.

It’s 18 months since Alexander-Arnold made the controversial decision to leave his boyhood club Liverpool for the Bernabeu.

The right-back signed a six-year contract, with the Reds receiving a paltry £8.5 million fee from the Spanish giants.

Trent played 30 matches for Real Madrid during his debut campaign in Spain but failed to win a trophy. He provided five assists but ended the season without a goal.

The appointment of Jose Mourinho as Real Madrid head coach in the summer left Alexander-Arnold’s future in doubt, particularly after the club splashed out £17 million to sign another right-back: Denzel Dumfries from Inter Milan.

While Trent, one of the best English footballers in the world, possesses many extraordinary attacking qualities, it was anticipated by many that the Englishman’s defensive frailties would cost him game time under the revered Portuguese tactician.

And that’s largely been the case during the opening months of the 2026-27 campaign. Trent has started only five of Real Madrid’s 13 league matches this term and has been an unused substitute on five occasions.

He only played one minute during October’s Clasico against Barcelona, and has featured in just one of Real Madrid’s four Champions League fixtures.

The 28-year-old was handed a start against Celta Vigo on Saturday but lasted just 57 minutes before Mourinho subbed him off.

Celta took the lead through Pablo Duran after just six minutes, with the goal coming down Real Madrid’s right-hand side.

Kylian Mbappe spurned the chance to equalise from the penalty spot midway though the first half and Trent was one of three Real Madrid players withdrawn, along with Alvaro Carreras and Bernardo Silva, with just over 30 minutes remaining.

Mourinho’s changes had the desired effect. Real Madrid won the match 2-1 thanks to goals from Jude Bellingham and Carlos Espi.

But make no mistake, this was a difficult evening for Alexander-Arnold, who was booed by his own fans at one point during the match.

The highest-paid English footballers in the world have been listed, including Harry Kane, Jude Bellingham and Bukayo Saka.

Per Liverpool.com, Spanish outlet Marca handed Trent an unflattering 4/10 rating, highlighting how a “lack of precision in attack” - noted as his “strong point” - failed to make up for his defensive weaknesses on this occasion.

“Too closed down on the play that led to Celta’s goal, having to cover a lot of ground to block Javi Galan’s run,” Marca continued.

“A failed cross from a good position to create danger. Another poor pass from an excellent position to put a dangerous ball ahead of the defence.”

“He continued to struggle after the restart and Mou [Mourinho] took him off in the 57th minute, bringing on Fede [Valverde]. A bad day for the Englishman.”

AS added: “He was caught out again, this time during Celta’s goal.

“Mourinho stuck with him until the 57th minute and he received some boos from the Bernabeu crowd when his name was announced as a substitute.

“His face and body language, completely expressionless, didn’t help matters either.”

Forget 'Bosch,' Netflix's 5-Part Detective Drama Is a Perfect Binge

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Prime Video's Bosch was one of the most exciting police procedurals on television. For seven seasons, from 2014 to 2021, we got to follow along with Los Angeles Police Department Detective Harry Bosch (Titus Welliver) as he investigated some seriously fascinating cases. The series has inspired two spin-off shows, including the three-season Bosch: Legacy and Ballard starring Maggie Q. MGM+ will be capitalizing on Bosch's devoted fanbase with another spin-off, a prequel called Bosch: Start of Watch, which is set to arrive in January. If you're looking for more in the vein of Bosch, there's another compelling detective drama, and it's one you might not even know about.

There have been a lot of cop shows in the history of television. But few of them have been as well-made as Southland. The underrated series, which ran for five seasons from 2009 to 2013, is a captivating look at law enforcement in Los Angeles. The drama, created by Ann Biderman (Ray Donovan), is a brutally realistic portrayal of the many complicated aspects of working in the LAPD. We gain entry into this high-stakes world by following the story of several LAPD officers, including rookie Officer Ben Sherman (Ben McKenzie). Ben is brand-new to the force, and is completely in the dark about the danger he's just walked into. His training officer, John Cooper (Michael Cudlitz), tries to show him the ropes, but as a closeted gay man, John is desperate to keep his personal life hidden from his co-workers. We also meet two inspirational women in the LAPD: Detective Lydia Adams (Regina King, pre-Oscar win) and Officer Chickie Brown (Arija Bareikis), who are both facing an uphill battle to make their way in a male-dominated field. Each character provides valuable insight into the complex life of these public servants.

Unlike some other cop shows, Southland creates an altogether authentic picture of the LAPD. There are the typical car chases and fight sequences, but these moments of high-intensity action serve the storytelling in a realistic way. The drama explores everything from gang violence and corrupt cops to the somber mental health and addiction struggles the officers face. The gritty style of Southland ensures that none of the storylines ever feel cheesy or predictable. The series also digs into the lives of these characters, so that they become fully dimensional people. The writing allows the viewer to feel the depth of their sorrow and traumas, as well as to celebrate some of their wins. It's easy to feel drawn into the world of Southland from the very first scene, which means the series is perfect for a binge-watch as you'll want to zoom ahead to catch every twisty episode.

Before he was Dr. Abbot, Shawn Hatosy was Detective Sammy Bryant.

The writing in Southland ensures there are zero bad episodes, but one of the most remarkable elements of the series is its extraordinary cast. McKenzie is spot-on as the naive Ben, and his scenes with Cudlitz crackle with tense electricity. King is a perfect addition to the cast, and her performance as Lydia provides a certain grounded feeling to the series. Shawn Hatosy, who recently won an Emmy for his work in The Pitt, brings a necessary frantic energy to his role as Sammy Bryant, an officer who can't seem to get out of his own way. C. Thomas Howell is also a bright spot in the cast as Bill "Dewey" Dudek. Howell provides much of the comic relief for the otherwise tonally dark series. In Season 4, Lucy Liu joined the cast as Officer Jessica Tang, delivering a riveting performance. The entire cast serves as a strong foundation that allows the storytelling to flourish, even when the subject matter is incredibly heavy.

For a while, it seemed like Southland would have a short run. Despite a second season renewal, NBC decided to cancel the show after Season 1. Luckily, TNT swooped in, and aired several more seasons. In 2013, TNT announced that Southland would be cancelled after five seasons, with no reprise in sight. Despite the fact that the show could have carried on for much longer, there are still 43 brilliant episodes for you to binge as a Bosch replacement.

Southland is available to stream on Netflix in the U.S.
