Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Employer Provided IUL

📅 Nov 22, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

I have wondered if that would be a good move. What I can glean from the illustration is:
• Withdrawals come out of cash surrender value, which is policy value less surrender charges. The illustrated scenario shows $0 after year 1, about $53k after year 3, and about $131k after year 5. That compares with $200k of premiums paid by year 5.
• Surrender charges apply for the first 9 years. The illustration says charges aren't deducted when withdrawals reduce the specified amount, so the cap is the cash surrender value. (?)
-It also states withdrawals in the first 15 years may incur additional fees and even be taxable. (?)

Also a 2% withdrawal fee, but I think it's capped at $25.
Thanks for posting some additional details. There are several different layers to unpack.

First, you note that $200k of premiums would be paid through year 5, which I assume are paid at the rate of $40k per year. But the cash surrender value is only $131k at the end of the 5th year. Unfortunately, that's typical for a "permanent" life insurance product like indexed universal life. I expect that if you look further out on the illustration, you'll see that the surrender value equals the cumulative premiums paid by about year 10. This cost is solely the result of your employer deciding to "pay" you with an IUL product rather than cash. (Also, the agent, who I expect is the friend of somebody highly placed in your company, probably made somewhere in the range of $40k in commissions for selling this policy.)

Second, while I stand by my suggestion of doing the simplest indexing strategy of S&P 1 year point to point with a cap, I don't feel that so strongly as to preclude other choices. All of those options should have generally the same "expected value", so pick whichever you want.

Third, upon reflection, I'd like to suggest an alternative way to factor this policy into your overall financial picture, presuming that you're able to partially surrender the cash surrender value while you remain employed. Central to this approach is realizing that the surrender value is really a "fixed income" investment (that is, not an "equity" investment, since you're credited with an interest rate each year which happens to be based on an external index with a floor of zero.)

As to this though process - I'd pick an indexing strategy, and then just let the policy sit and accumulate value for maybe 5 years or so, being careful to retain the annual statements that you'll get shortly after each policy anniversary. At the end of 5 years, the illustration shows that you should have $131k of surrender value. At that time, you can go through a two-step thought process, as follows ----
---- How does my overall asset allocation look, including this $131k of surrender value as a "fixed income" asset? If, for example, your targeted asset allocation were to be 100% equities at this stage in your life, that would suggest that you should withdraw cash from the policy and reinvest the proceeds in equities, ignoring income tax consequences (discussed below). On the other hand, if I want to have this $131k invested in fixed income assets, then go to step 2 below.
---- Does the rate I'm earning on the IUL surrender value look good compared to alternatives? You've now got 5 years of interest credit history, so you can do a geometric average of the five credited rates. There's a chance that the geometric average after 5 years will be somewhere in the range of 5-7% per year, which you may find attractive compared to alternatives. However, if the geometric average is pretty low, it would be in your best interest to partially surrender the policy and reinvest the proceeds in some other fixed income asset outside the IUL, subject to income tax consequences.

As to income tax - presuming that the premiums are paid fully by the employer, I would expect that any partial surrender would be taxed as ordinary income to you. (Note that I am not a tax expert.) No matter what the rate of return on the IUL policy is, it might not be advantageous for you to take withdrawals during your working lifetime while your marginal income tax rate is much higher than it will be in retirement. Further, if you were to keep this policy until you die, I do not know whether your beneficiaries would need to pay income tax on the death benefit proceeds.

Sorry to give such a long answer to your relatively short question. And I'm really sorry that your employer isn't just giving you a $40k annual raise rather than giving you an IUL policy. But that's your situation.

Post back with any questions. And best to you.
